4.2
Putting everything together

We adapt the construction of
Section

4.1
to propagate operations on counters of the machine issued by the head of the line. Counters processes will evolve in three different protocols as in
Section

4.1
.
They can be either in a
zero
state, from which all the types of instructions can be transmitted, or in a state
1_{\textsf{x}}
for
x
one of the
two counters, from which all the types of operations can be transmitted, except 0-tests of
x
.
Increments and decrements of a counter
x
are done in a similar fashion as in
Section

4.1
(exactly one node changes its state).
0-tests are somewhat easier: no node changes state nor executes anything, and the tail accepts the same pair as the one broadcast by the head. However, if a node is in a
1_{\textsf{x}}
when
x
is the counter compared to 0, it fails when its left neighbor broadcasts the request.

We ensure that we can select a line with a similar structure as the one depicted in
Figure

9
thanks to a first part of the protocol where each node:
(i)
receives an announcement message from its predecessor with a subscript
j
(except the head which broadcasts first),
(ii)
broadcasts an announcement message with the subscript
(j+1)\mod 3
(head broadcasts with subscript 0) and
(iii)
waits for the announcement of its successor with subscript
(j+2)\mod 3
(except for the tail). If it receives any new announcement at any point of its execution, it fails. When considering only line topologies, as each node has at most two neighbors, this part can be achieved with fewer alternations.
We get the two following theorems.

Theorem 9
.

Cover
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
are undecidable for
k
-phase-bounded protocols with
k\geq 6
.

Theorem 10
.

{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Lines}]}
is undecidable for
k
-phase-bounded protocols with
k\geq 4
.

5
Cover
in 1-Phase-Bounded Protocols

We show that
\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Graphs}]
restricted to 1-phase-bounded
protocols is
ExpSpace
-complete.

We begin by proving that
for such protocols
\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Graphs}]
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Stars}]}
are equivalent (where

Stars
correspond to the tree topologies of height one). To get
this property, we first rely on Theorem

4
(stating that
Cover
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
are equivalent)
and without loss of generality we can assume that if a
control state can be covered with a tree topology, it can be
covered by the root of the tree. We then observe that when dealing
with 1-phase-bounded protocols, the behaviour of the processes of a tree
which are located at a height strictly greater than 1 have no incidence on the
root node. Indeed if a process at depth 2 performs a broadcast received by a node at
depth 1, then this latter node will not be able to influence the state of the
root because in 1-phase-bounded protocols, once a process has performed
a reception, it cannot broadcast anymore.
In the sequel we fix a 1-phase-bounded protocol
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
and a state
q_{f}\in Q
. We then have:

Lemma 11
.

There exist
\Gamma\in\textsf{Graphs}
,
C=(\Gamma,L)\in\mathcal{I}_{P}
and
D=(\Gamma,L^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
and
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}D
and
L^{\prime}(v)=q_{f}
iff there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\textsf{Stars}
,
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{I}
and
D^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
such that
C^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}_{P}D^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime\prime\prime}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
.

To solve
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Stars}]}
in
ExpSpace
, we
proceed as follows (1) we first propose an abstract representation
for the configurations reachable by executions where
the root node does not perform any reception, and that only keeps track of
states in
Q_{0}
and
Q^{b}_{1}
(2) we show that we can decide in
polynomial space whether a configuration corresponding to a given abstract representation can be reached from an initial
configuration (3) relying on reduction to the control state reachability
problem in VASS (Vector Addition System with States), we show how to
decide whether there exists a configuration corresponding to a given abstract
representation from which
q_{f}
can be covered in an execution where
the root node does not perform any broadcast. This reasoning relies on
the fact that a process executing a 1-phase-bounded protocol first
performs only broadcast (or internal actions) and then performs only
receptions (or internal actions).

We use
Q^{b}
to represent the set
Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
and we say that a
configuration
C=(\Gamma,L)
in
\mathcal{C}_{P}
is a
star-configuration

whenever
\Gamma\in\textsf{Stars}
. For a star-configuration
C=(\Gamma,L)
in
\mathcal{C}_{P}
such that

L(\epsilon)\in Q^{b}
, the
broadcast-print
of
C
, denoted
by
\mathbf{bprint}(C)
, is the pair
(L(\epsilon),\{L(v)\in Q^{b}\mid v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus\{\epsilon\}\})
in
Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
. We call such a configuration
C
a b-configuration. Note
that any initial star-configuration
C_{in}=(\Gamma_{in},L_{in})\in\mathcal{I}
is a b-configuration verifying
\mathbf{bprint}(C_{in})\in\{(q_{in},\emptyset),(q_{in},\{q_{in}\})\}
(the first case
corresponding to
\textsf{V}(\Gamma)=\{\epsilon\}
). We now
define a transition relation
\Rightarrow
between
broadcast-prints. Given
(q,\Lambda)
and
(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
in
Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
, we write
(q,\Lambda)\Rightarrow(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
if there
exists two
b
-configurations
C
and
C^{\prime}
such that

\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime})=(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
and
C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
. We denote by
\Rightarrow^{\ast}
the reflexive and
transitive closure of
\Rightarrow
.

One interesting point of this abstract representation
is that we can compute in polynomial time the
\Rightarrow
-successor
of a given broadcast-print. The intuition is simple:
either the root performs a broadcast of
m\in\Sigma
, and in that case we have to
remove from the set
\Lambda
all the states from which a reception of

m
can be done (as the associated processes in
C^{\prime}
will not be in a
state in
Q^{b}
anymore) or one process in a state of
\Lambda

performs a broadcast and in that case it should not be received by the
root node (otherwise the reached configuration will not be a
b-configuration anymore).

Lemma 12
.

Given
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
, we can compute in polynomial time the set
\{(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\mid(q,\Lambda)\Rightarrow(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\}
.

In order to show that our abstract representation can be used to solve

{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Stars}]}
, we need to rely on some further formal definitions. Given
two star-configurations
C=(\Gamma,L)
and
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime})
,
we write
C\preceq C^{\prime}
iff the two following conditions hold
(i)

L(\epsilon)=L^{\prime}(\epsilon)
, and,
(ii)

|\{v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus\{\epsilon\}\mid L(v)=q\}|\leq|\{v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{\epsilon\}\mid L^{\prime}(v)=q\}|
for
all
q\in Q^{b}
. We then have the following lemma where the two
first points show that when dealing with star-configurations, the
network generated by
1
-phase-bounded protocol enjoys some
monotonicity properties. Indeed, if
the root node performs a broadcast received by other nodes, then
if we put more nodes in the same state, they will also
receive the message. On the other hand if it is another node that
performs a broadcast, only the root node is able to receive it. The last point of the lemma shows that we
can have as many processes as we want in reachable states in
Q^{b}

(as soon as the root node does not perform any reception) by
duplicating nodes and mimicking behaviors.

Lemma 13
.

The following properties hold:

(i)

If
C_{1}
,
C^{\prime}_{1}
and
C_{2}
are star-configurations such that
C_{1}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{1}
and
C_{1}\preceq C_{2}
then there
exists a star-configuration
C^{\prime}_{2}
such that
C_{1}^{\prime}\preceq C^{\prime}_{2}
and
C_{2}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{2}
.

(ii)

If
C_{1}
,
C^{\prime}_{1}
and
C_{2}
are
b-configurations such that
C_{1}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathbf{bprint}(C_{1})=\mathbf{bprint}(C_{2})
and
C_{1}\preceq C_{2}
then there
exists a b-configuration
C^{\prime}_{2}
such that
C_{1}^{\prime}\preceq C^{\prime}_{2}
and

\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{1})=\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{2})
and
C_{2}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{2}
.

(iii)

If
C
is a b-configuration such that
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C
for some initial configuration
C_{in}
then for all
N\in{\rm Nature}
,
there exists an initial configuration
C^{\prime}_{in}
and a b-configuration
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime})
such that
C^{\prime}_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime})=(q,\Lambda)
and

|\{v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{\epsilon\}\mid L^{\prime}(v)=q^{\prime}\}|\geq N
for all
q^{\prime}\in\Lambda
.

We can now prove that we
can reason in a sound and complete way with broadcast prints to
characterise the b-configurations reachable from initial star-configurations. To prove this next lemma, we rely on the two last
points of the previous lemma and reason by induction on the length
of the
\Rightarrow
-path leading from
(q_{in},\Lambda_{in})
to
(q,\Lambda)
.

Lemma 14
.

Given
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
, we have
(q_{in},\Lambda_{in})\Rightarrow^{\ast}(q,\Lambda)

with
\Lambda_{in}\in\{\emptyset,\{q_{in}\}\}
iff there exist
two
b
-configurations
C_{in}\in\mathcal{I}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}
such
that
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
.

Finally, we show that we can verify in exponential space
whether there exists a configuration with a given broadcast-print
(q,\Lambda)

from which we can reach a configuration covering
q_{f}
thanks to an
execution where the root node does not perform any broadcast. This result is obtained
by a reduction to the control state
reachability problem in (unary) VASS
which is known to be
ExpSpace
-complete
[
17
,
20
]
. VASS are
finite state machines equipped with variables (called counters) taking their values in
{\rm Nature}
,
and where each transition of the machine
can either change the value of a counter, by incrementing or
decrementing it, or do nothing. In our reduction, we encode the state
of the root in the control state of the VASS and we associate a
counter to each state of
Q^{b}
to represent the number of processes in
this state. In a first phase,
the VASS generates a configuration with
(q,\Lambda)
as
broadcast-print and in a second phase it simulates the network. For
instance, if a process performs a broadcast received by the root node,
then we decrement the counter associated to the source state of the
broadcast, we increment the one associated to the target state
and we change the control state of the VASS representing the state of the
root node accordingly. We need a last definition to characterise executions
where the root node does not perform any broadcast: given two star-configurations

C=(\Gamma,L)
and
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime})
, we write
C\xrightarrow{}_{r}C^{\prime}

whenever there exist
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
and
\delta\in\Delta

such that
C\xrightarrow{v,\delta}C^{\prime}
and either
v\neq\epsilon
or

\delta=(q,\tau,q^{\prime})
for some
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
. We denote by

\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}
the reflexive and transitive closure of

\rightarrow_{r}
.

Lemma 15
.

Given
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
, we can decide in
ExpSpace
whether
there exist a b-configuration
C=(\Gamma_{f},L)
and a star-configuration

C_{f}=(\Gamma_{f},L_{f})
such that
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
and
L_{f}(\epsilon)=q_{f}

and
C\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}C_{f}
.

Combining the results of the previous lemmas leads to an

ExpSpace
-algorithm to solve
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Stars}]}
. We first guess a
broadcast-print
(q,\Lambda)
and check in polynomial space whether it is

\Rightarrow
-reachable from an initial broadcast-print in

\{(q_{in},\emptyset),(q_{in},\{q_{in}\})\}
thanks to Lemma

12
(relying on a non-deterministic
polynomial space algorithm for reachability). Then we use

Lemma

15
to check the existence of a b-configuration
C
with
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
from which we can cover

q_{f}
. By Savitch’s theorem
[
22
]
, we conclude that the problem is in
ExpSpace
.
The completeness of this method is direct. For the soundess, we reason as follows: using Lemma

14
, there exists a configuration
C

reachable from an initial star-configuration such that

\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
, and by
Lemma

15
, there is a configuration
C^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime})=(q,\Lambda)

from which we cover
q_{f}
. Thanks to

Lemma

13
.
(iii)
, there is a configuration
C^{\prime\prime}
reachable
from an initial configuration such that
C\preceq C^{\prime\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\preceq C^{\prime\prime}
and

\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime\prime})=(q,\Lambda)
. Thanks to

Lemma

13
.
(i)
applied to each transition, we can build an execution from
C^{\prime\prime}
that covers

q_{f}
. The lower bound is obtained by a reduction from the
control state reachability in VASS.

Theorem 16
.

\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Graphs}]
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
are
ExpSpace
-complete for 1-phase-bounded protocols.

6
Decidability Results for 2-Phase-Bounded Protocols

6.1
Cover
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
are Decidable on 2-PB Protocols

A
simple path between
u
and
u^{\prime}
in a topology
\Gamma=(V,E)
is a sequence of distinct vertices
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}
such that
u=v_{0}
,
u^{\prime}=v_{k}
, and for all
0\leq i<k
,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E
. Its length is denoted
d(v_{0},\dots,v_{k})
and is equal to
k
. Given an integer
K
, we say that a topology
\Gamma
is
K
-bounded path (and we write
\Gamma\in K-\textsf{BP}
) if there is no simple path
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}
such that
d(v_{0},\dots,v_{k})>K

The result of this subsection relies on the following theorem.

Theorem 17

(
[
6
]
,Theorem 5)
.

For
K\geq 1
,
Cover[
K
-
BP
]
is decidable.

Hence, we show that if a state
q_{f}
of a protocol
P
is coverable with a tree topology, then
q_{f}
is actually coverable with a tree topology that is
also
2(|Q|+1)-\textsf{BP}
.

To establish this result, consider a coverable state
q_{f}
of a protocol
P
with a tree topology
\Gamma
, such that
\Gamma
is minimal in the number of nodes needed to cover
q_{f}
. We can suppose wlog that
q_{f}
is covered by the root of the tree. We argue that all nodes (except maybe the root) in the execution covering
q_{f}
broadcast something, as otherwise they are useless and could then be removed. We also argue that, since
P
is 2-phase-bounded, a node that would first broadcast after the first broadcast of its father would also be useless for the covering of
q_{f}
: this broadcast will only be received by its father in its
last phase of reception
, hence it will have no influence on the behavior of the root.
These two properties are the key elements needed to establish the following lemma.

Lemma 18
.

Let
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
be a 2-phase-bounded protocol and
q_{f}\in Q
. If
q_{f}
can be covered with a tree topology, then it can be covered with a topology
\Gamma\in\textsf{Trees}
such that, for all
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
,
|u|\leq|Q|+1
.

Indeed, a counting argument implies that if this is not the case, there exist two nodes
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
on the same branch, different from the root, with
u_{1}

a prefix of
u_{2}
, that both execute their first broadcast from the same state
q
.
In this case, we could replace the subtree rooted in
u_{1}
by the subtree rooted in
u_{2}
, and still obtain an execution covering
q_{f}
. Once
u_{1}
has
reached
q
(possibly by receiving broadcasts from the children of
u_{2}
), it will behave as in the initial execution. Behaviors of the children of
u_{1}
might differ in this second part, but it can only influence
u_{1}
in its reception phase, which will be the last phase, and hence will not influence the behavior of the root.

Thanks to
Theorems

4
and
17
, we can then conclude.

Theorem 19
.

Cover
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
are decidable for 2-phase-bounded protocols.

6.2
Polynomial Time Algorithm for
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Lines}]}
on 2-PB Protocols

In the rest of this section, we fix a 2-phase-bounded protocol
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
and a state
q_{f}\in Q
to cover.
For an execution
\rho=C_{0}\xrightarrow{}C_{1}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\xrightarrow{}C_{n}
with
C_{n}=(\Gamma,L_{n})
, for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, we denote by
b_{\textsf{first}}(v,\rho)
the
smallest
index
0\leq i<n
such that
C_{i}\xrightarrow{v,t}C_{i+1}
with
t=(q,!!m,q^{\prime})\in\Delta
.
If
v
never broadcasts anything,
b_{\textsf{first}}(v,\rho)=-1
. We also denote by
t_{\textsf{last}}(v,\rho)
the
largest
index
0\leq i<n
,
such that
C_{i}\xrightarrow{v,t}C_{i+1}
for some transition
t\in\Delta
. If
v
never issues any transition, we let
t_{\textsf{last}}(v,\rho)=-1
.

The polynomial time algorithm relies on the fact that to cover a state, one can consider only executions that have a specific shape, described in the following lemma.

Lemma 20
.

If
q_{f}
is coverable with a line topology
\Gamma
such that
\textsf{V}(\Gamma)=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}\}
then there exists an execution
\rho=C_{0}\xrightarrow{}C_{1}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\xrightarrow{}C_{n}
such that
C_{n}=(\Gamma,L_{n})
,
and
3\leq N\leq\ell-2
with
L_{n}(v_{N})=q_{f}
, and

1.

there exist
0\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<n
such that for all
0\leq j<n
, if we let
C_{j}\xrightarrow{v^{j},t^{j}}C_{j+1}
:

(a)
if
0\leq j<j_{1}
, then
v^{j}\in\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N-2}\}
and if
v^{j}=v_{N-2}
, then
t^{j}=(q,\tau,q^{\prime})
for some
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
; and

(b)
if
j_{1}\leq j<j_{2}
, then
v^{j}\in\{v_{N+2},\dots,v_{\ell}\}
and if
v^{j}=v_{N+2}
, then
t^{j}=(q,\tau,q^{\prime})
for some
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
; and

(c)
if
j_{2}\leq j<n
, then
v^{j}\in\{v_{N-2},\dots,v_{N+2}\}
.

2.

(a)
for all
1\leq i\leq N-2
,
t_{\textsf{last}}(v_{i},\rho)\leq b_{\textsf{first}}(v_{i+1},\rho)
, and

(b)
for all
N+2\leq i\leq\ell
,
t_{\textsf{last}}(v_{i},\rho)\leq b_{\textsf{first}}(v_{i-1},\rho)
.

v_{1}:
q_{in}
v_{N-2}:
q_{in}
v_{N-1}:
q_{in}
v_{N}:
q_{in}
v_{N+1}:
q_{in}
v_{N+2}:
q_{in}
v_{\ell}:
q_{in}
…
…
\ast
C_{0}
v_{1}:
\_
v_{N-2}:
q_{1}
v_{N-1}:
q_{in}
v_{N}:
q_{in}
v_{N+1}:
q_{in}
v_{N+2}:
q_{in}
v_{\ell}:
q_{in}
…
…
\ast
C_{j_{1}}
v_{1}:
\_
v_{N-2}:
q_{1}
v_{N-1}:
q_{in}
v_{N}:
q_{in}
v_{N+1}:
q_{in}
v_{N+2}:
q_{2}
v_{\ell}:
\_
…
…
\ast
C_{j_{2}}
v_{1}:
\_
v_{N-2}:
\_
v_{N-1}:
\_
v_{N}:
q_{f}
v_{N+1}:
\_
v_{N+2}:
\_
v_{\ell}:
\_
…
…
C_{n}
no broadcast from
v_{N-2}
no broadcast from
v_{N+2}

Figure 12
:
Illustration of execution
\rho
obtained from
Lemma

20
.

Figure

12
illustrates the specific form of the execution described in
Item

1
of
Lemma

20
: the first nodes to take actions are the ones in the purple part (on the left), then, only nodes in the green part (on the right) issue transitions), and finally the nodes in the orange central part take actions in order to reach
q_{f}
. The fact that
P
is 2-phase bounded allows us to
establish
Item

2
of
Lemma

20
: when
v_{i+1}
starts broadcasting, no further broadcasts from
v_{i}
will influence
v_{i+1}
’s broadcasts (it can only receive them in its last reception phase).

Figure

12
highlights why we get a polynomial time algorithm: when we reach the orange part of the execution, the nodes
v_{N-1}
,
v_{N}

and
v_{N+1}
are still in the initial state of the protocol. Moreover, in the orange part (which is the one that witnesses the covering of
q_{f}
), only five nodes take
actions. Once one has computed in which set of states the nodes
v_{N-2}
and
v_{N+2}
can be at the beginning of the orange part, it only remains to compute
the set of reachable configurations from a finite set of configurations. Let
H
be the set of possible states in which
v_{N-2}
and
v_{N+2}
can be at the
beginning of the last part of the execution, and for
q_{1},q_{2}\in H
, let
C_{q_{1},q_{2}}=(\Gamma_{5},L_{q_{1},q_{2}})
where
\Gamma_{5}
is the line topology
with five vertices
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},v_{5}\}
and
L_{q_{1},q_{2}}(v_{1})=q_{1}
,
L_{q_{1},q_{2}}(v_{5})=q_{2}
and for all other vertex
v
,
L_{q_{1},q_{2}}(v)=q_{in}
.

Our algorithm is then: (1) Compute
H
; (2) For all
q_{1},q_{2}\in H
, explore reachable configurations from
C_{q_{1},q_{2}}
; (3) Answer yes if we reach a configuration covering
q_{f}
, answer no otherwise.
It remains to explain how to compute
H
. This computation relies on
Item

2
of
Lemma

20
:
locally, each node
v_{i}
at the left of
v_{N-1}
(resp. at the right of
v_{N+1}
) stops issuing transitions once its right neighbor
v_{i+1}
(resp. its left neighbor
v_{i-1}
) starts broadcasting.

Hence we compute iteratively set of coverable pairs of states
S\subseteq Q\times Q
by relying on a family
(S_{i})_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
of subsets of
Q\times Q
formally defined as follows:

\displaystyle S_{0}=\{(q_{in},q_{in})\}

\displaystyle S_{i+1}=S_{i}\ \cup\{(q_{1},q_{2})\mid\textrm{there exist }(p_{1},p_{2})\in S_{i},j\in\{1,2\}\textrm{ s.t. }(p_{j},\tau,q_{j}) \in\Delta\text{ and }p_{3-j}=q_{3-j}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q_{1},q_{2})\mid\textrm{there exists }(p_{1},p_{2})\in S_{i},\textrm{ s.t. }(p_{2},!!m,q_{2}) \in\Delta,(p_{1},?m,q_{1}) \in\Delta,m\in\Sigma\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q_{1},q_{2})\mid\textrm{there exists }p_{2}\in Q\textrm{ s.t. }(q_{1},p_{2})\in S_{i},\textrm{ and }(p_{2},!!m,q_{2}) \in\Delta\textrm{ and }m\nin R(q_{1})\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q_{in},q)\mid\textrm{there exists }(q,q^{\prime})\in S_{i} \textrm{ for some }q^{\prime}\in Q\}.

We then define
S=\bigcup_{n \in{\rm Nature}}S_{n}
, and
H=\{q\in Q\mid\textrm{ there exists }q^{\prime}\textrm{ and }(q,q^{\prime})\in S\}
.
Observe that
(S_{i})_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
is an increasing sequence bounded by
|Q|^{2}
. The computation reaches then a fixpoint and
S
can be computed in polynomial time.
We define
H=\{q\mid\exists q^{\prime}\in Q,(q,q^{\prime})\in S\}
. Note that
H\subseteq Q_{0}\cup Q_{1}^{r}
, as expected by
Item

2
of
Lemma

20
.

We also state that our construction is complete and correct, leading to the following theorem.

Theorem 21
.

{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Lines}]}
is in P for
k
-phase-bounded protocols with
k\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

We explain why the algorithm takes a polynomial time: step 1 (computing
H
) is done in polynomial time as explained above. For step 2, there are at most
|H|\times|H|\leq|Q|^{2}
pairs, and for each pair, we explore a graph of at most
|Q|^{5}
nodes in which each vertex represents a configuration
C=(\Gamma_{5},L)
. Accessibility in a graph can be done non-deterministically in logarithmic space, and so in polynomial time.
Observe that all the lemmas of this section hold true when considering 1-phase-bounded protocols, hence the theorem.
∎

Appendix A
Cover
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
are equivalent

Let
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
be a broadcast protocol, and
q_{f}\in Q
. Let
\rho=C_{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{n}
with
C_{i}=(\Gamma,L_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
for all
0\leq i\leq n
, and a vertex
v_{f}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
such that
L_{n}(v_{f})=q_{f}
.
We will build an execution covering
q_{f}
with a tree topology
\Gamma^{\prime}
, rooted in
v_{f}
, the node that reaches
q_{f}
.

\Gamma^{\prime}
is actually an
unfolding
of the topology
\Gamma
.

We first define inductively the set of nodes
V^{\prime}\subseteq{\rm Nature}^{\ast}
, along with a labelling function
\lambda
which associates to each node
v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
a node
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
.

1.

\epsilon\in V^{\prime}
and
\lambda(\epsilon)=v_{f}
;

2.

Let
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(v_{f})=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. For all
1\leq i\leq k
,
i\in V^{\prime}
and
\lambda(i)=v_{i}
;

3.

Let
w\cdot x\in V^{\prime}
, with
w\in{\rm Nature}^{\ast}
such that
|w|<n-1
, and
x\in{\rm Nature}
. Let
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(w\cdot x))\setminus\{\lambda(w)\}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. Then, for all
1\leq i\leq k
,
w\cdot x\cdot i\in V^{\prime}
and
\lambda^{\prime}(w\cdot x\cdot i)=v_{i}
.

Finally, define
E^{\prime}=\{\langle w,w\cdot x\rangle\mid w\in V^{\prime},w\cdot x\in V^{\prime}\}
.
Note that
\epsilon\in V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}\subseteq{\rm Nature}^{\ast}
and is prefix closed.
Furthermore, by construction, for all
w\in V^{\prime}
,
|w|\leq n
, and for all
w\in V^{\prime}
, in fact
w\in\{1,\dots,d\}^{\ast}

where
d
is the maximal degree of
\Gamma
. Hence,
V^{\prime}
is a finite set and
\Gamma^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a tree topology.

The way we built
\Gamma^{\prime}
ensures that each node
v\in V^{\prime}
(except the leaves) enjoys the same set of neighbors than
\lambda(v)\in V
. This is formalised in the
following lemma.

Lemma 22
.

For all
u\in V^{\prime}
, for all
u^{\prime}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
,
\lambda(u^{\prime})\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(u))
. Then, we let
f_{u}:\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)\rightarrow\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(u))
defined by
f_{u}(u^{\prime})=\lambda(u^{\prime})
. If
|u|<n
,
f_{u}

is a bijection.

Proof.

We prove the lemma inductively on the structure of the nodes of
V^{\prime}
.

•

Let
u=\epsilon
, then
\lambda(u)=v_{f}
and let
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(v_{f})=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. Then by definition, for all
1\leq i\leq k
,
i\in V^{\prime}

and
\lambda(i)=v_{i}
. By definition of
E^{\prime}
,
\{1,\dots,k\}\subseteq\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\epsilon)
. Now let
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\epsilon)
, again by definition of

E^{\prime}
,
|u|=1
and
u\in\{1,\dots,k\}
because all other nodes added in
V^{\prime}
are of length greater of equal than 2. So
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\epsilon)=\{1,\dots,k\}
, and the function
f_{\epsilon}
such that
f_{\epsilon}(i)=\lambda(i)=v_{i}
is obviously a bijection.

•

Let
w\cdot x\in V^{\prime}
such that
|w\cdot x|<n
and
w\in{\rm Nature}^{\ast}
. By induction hypothesis,
f_{w}:\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(w)\rightarrow\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(w))
is a bijection.
Hence, since by definition of
E^{\prime}
,
w\cdot x\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(w)
,
f_{w}(w\cdot x)=\lambda(w\cdot x)\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(w))
and
(\lambda(w),\lambda(w\cdot x))\in E
.
Let then
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(w\cdot x))=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell},\lambda(w)\}
. By definition of
V^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
,
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(w\cdot x)=\{w,w\cdot x\cdot 1,\dots,w\cdot x\cdot\ell\}
and
\lambda(w\cdot x\cdot i)=v_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Hence,
f_{w\cdot x}
is a bijection.

Moreover, if
w\cdot x\in V^{\prime}
with
|w\cdot x|=n
, then
w\cdot x
is a leaf and
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(w\cdot x)=\{w\}
. By construction,
\lambda(w\cdot x)=v\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(w))
, which implies that
\lambda(w)\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(w\cdot x))
.
∎

From the execution
\rho=C_{0}\rightarrow\dots C_{n}
we will build a similar execution on
\Gamma^{\prime}
. The idea is that for each step of the execution
\rho
,
for each node
v\in V
, all the nodes in
\Gamma^{\prime}
that are labelled by
v
will behave in the same way. This is possible thanks to
Lemma

22
.
Observe though that the leaves might no be able to behave as expected because they might not have the same set of neighbors than the node they are labelled by, so they might not be able to receive some
broadcast message. However, we can ensure some weaker version of correctness, defined as follows.
Let
0\leq h\leq n
and
C=(\Gamma,L)
be a configuration. We say that a configuration
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime})
is
h
-correct
for
C
if for all
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, if
|u|\leq h
then
L^{\prime}(u)=L(\lambda(u))
.

The following lemma gives the main ingredient that allows to mimick the execution
\rho
on
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Lemma 23
.

Let
C_{1}=(\Gamma,L_{1})
,
C_{2}=(\Gamma,L_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
such that
C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
and let
C^{\prime}_{1}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
and
0<h\leq n
such that
C^{\prime}_{1}
is
h
-correct for
C_{1}
.
There exists
C^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
such that
C^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{2}
, and
C^{\prime}_{2}
is
(h-1)
-correct for
C_{2}
.

Proof.

Denote
C_{1}\xrightarrow{v,t}C_{2}
with
t=(q,\alpha,q^{\prime})
and
\alpha\in!!\Sigma\cup\{\tau\}
.
Let
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{K}\}
be the set of vertices in
V^{\prime}
such that for all
1\leq k\leq K
,
|u_{k}|\leq h
and
\lambda(u_{k})=v
. We will build inductively an execution
C^{\prime}_{1,0}\xrightarrow{u_{1},t}C^{\prime}_{1,1}\xrightarrow{u_{2},t}\dots\xrightarrow{u_{K},t}C^{\prime}_{1,K}
of
configurations over
\Gamma^{\prime}

such that for all
0\leq k\leq K
,
C^{\prime}_{1,k}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1,k})
and for all node
u\in V^{\prime}
such that
|u|\leq h
,

•

if
u\in\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
, then
L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
,

•

otherwise,
L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1}(u)
.

We let
C^{\prime}_{1,0}=C^{\prime}_{1}
, since
\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))=\emptyset
,
C^{\prime}_{1}
trivially meets the requirements.

Let
0\leq k<K
and assume now that we have built
C^{\prime}_{1,0},\dots,C^{\prime}_{1,k}
as required. First, observe that

(u_{k+1}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1}))\cap(\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j})))=\emptyset.

(1)

Indeed assume otherwise and let
u
an element of this intersection. If
u=u_{k+1}
, then
u\notin\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}\{u_{j}\}
by definition. Then it must
be in
\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j})
and let
j
such that
u_{k+1}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j})
. By
Lemma

22
,

f_{u_{j}}(u_{k+1})=\lambda(u_{k+1})=v\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(\lambda(u_{j}))=\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(v)
. Hence it implies that
(v,v)\in E
which is impossible.
If
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
then by a similar argument,
u\notin\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}\{u_{j}\}
. Then let
1\leq j\leq k
such that
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j})
. Then
u_{j}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
and
u_{k+1}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
. If
|u|<n
(
u
is not a leaf), then
f_{u}(u_{j})=\lambda(u_{j})=v
and

f_{u}(u_{k+1})=\lambda(u_{k+1})=v
which contradicts the fact that
f_{u}
is bijective from
Lemma

22
. If
|u|=n
, then
it is a leaf and it has only one neighbor: its father in the tree. So it is not possible that
\{u_{j},u_{k+1}\}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
.

To define
C^{\prime}_{1,k+1}
we need to differentiate between
the possible values of
\alpha
(recall that
t=(q,\alpha,q^{\prime})
).

•

if
\alpha=\tau
, let
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u_{k+1})=q^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)
for all
u\neq u_{k+1}
. First, by
Equation

1
and induction hypothesis,

u_{k+1}\notin\bigcup(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
, then
L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u_{k+1})=L^{\prime}_{1}(u_{k+1})
. Moreover, since
C^{\prime}_{1}
is
h
-correct for

C_{1}
,
L^{\prime}_{1}(u_{k+1})=L_{1}(v)=q
. Then
C^{\prime}_{1,k}\xrightarrow{u_{k+1},t}C^{\prime}_{1,k+1}
. Let
u\in V^{\prime}
such that
|u|\leq h
and
u\in\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k+1}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
. If
u\in\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
, by induction hypothesis,
L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
. Moreover, by
Equation

1
,
u\neq u_{k+1}
. Then,

L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
. By construction,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u_{k+1})=q^{\prime}=L_{2}(\lambda(u_{k+1}))
. Now, if
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
,
or
u\notin\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k+1}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
.
Then,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)
. By induction hypothesis,
L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1}(u)=L_{1}(\lambda(u))
because
C^{\prime}_{1}
is
h
-correct for
C_{1}
. Moreover,

L_{1}(\lambda(u))=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
. Hence,
C^{\prime}_{1,k+1}
meets the requirements.

•

If
\alpha=!!m
for some
m\in\Sigma
, we define
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}
as follows.

–

L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u_{k+1})=q^{\prime}
(the node
u_{k+1}
performs the broadcast).

–

for all
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
such that
|u|\leq h
,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
.

–

for all
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
such that
|u|>h
, if there exists
p\in Q
and
(L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u),?m,p)\in\Delta
,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=p
, otherwise,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)
(its neighbors receive the broadcast).

–

For all other
u\in V^{\prime}
,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L_{1,k}(u)
.

We first show that
C^{\prime}_{1,k}\xrightarrow{u_{k+1},t}C^{\prime}_{1,k+1}
. By
Equation

1
,
u_{k+1}\notin\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
. Hence, the induction hypothesis ensures that
L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u_{k+1})=L_{1}(\lambda(u_{k+1})=L_{1}(v)
. Let
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
such that
|u|\leq h
. By
Equation

1
,

u\notin\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
. Then, by induction hypothesis,
L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1}(u)=L_{1}(\lambda(u))
since

C^{\prime}_{1}
is
h
-correct for
C_{1}
. Moreover, since
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
,
Lemma

22
implies that

\lambda(u)\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(v)
. Since
C_{1}\xrightarrow{v,t}C_{2}
, we know that either
(L_{1}(\lambda(u)),?m,L_{2}(\lambda(u)))\in\Delta
, hence

(L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u),?m,L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u))\in\Delta
,
or
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))=L_{1}(\lambda(u))=L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)
. In both cases, we can conclude that
C^{\prime}_{1,k}\xrightarrow{u_{k+1},t}C^{\prime}_{1,k+1}
.

Let now
u\in V^{\prime}
such that
|u|\leq h
and
u\in\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k+1}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
. If
u\in\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
, again by
Equation

1
,
u\notin\{u_{k+1}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
, and
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
by induction hypothesis. If
u=u_{k+1}
,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=q^{\prime}=L_{2}(v)=L_{2}(\lambda(u_{k+1})
. If
u\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{k+1})
, by definition we have that

L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
. If now
|u|\leq h
and
u\nin\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k+1}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
,
L^{\prime}_{1,k+1}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,k}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1}(u)
by induction hypothesis.

We let
C^{\prime}_{2}=C^{\prime}_{1,K}
and we prove that
C^{\prime}_{2}
is
(h-1)
-correct for
C_{2}
.
Let
u\in V^{\prime}
such that
|u|\leq h-1<n
. As
C^{\prime}_{1}
is
h
-correct for
C_{1}
,
L^{\prime}_{1}(u)=L_{1}(\lambda(u))
.

If
u
is such that
\lambda(u)\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(v)
, then by
Lemma

22
, there exists
u^{\prime}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
such that
\lambda(u^{\prime})=v
. Furthermore, since
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a tree topology, and
|u|\leq h-1
, either
|u^{\prime}|\leq h-2
or
|u^{\prime}|\leq h-1+1=h
. In both cases,
|u^{\prime}|\leq h
. As a consequence,
u^{\prime}\in\{u_{1},\dots,u_{K}\}
, and so,
L^{\prime}_{2}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,K}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
.
If
u
is such that
\lambda(u)=v
, then
u\in\{u_{1},\dots,u_{K}\}
, and again,
L^{\prime}_{2}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,K}(u)=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
.
In other cases,
\lambda(u)\nin\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(v)\cup\{v\}
, and
L_{2}(\lambda(u))=L_{1}(\lambda(u))
.
By definition of
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{K}\}
and by
Lemma

22
,
u\notin\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}(\{u_{j}\}\cup\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u_{j}))
. As a consequence,
L^{\prime}_{2}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1,K}(u)=L^{\prime}_{1}(u)=L_{1}(\lambda(u))=L_{2}(\lambda(u))
.

Hence,
C^{\prime}_{2}
is
(h-1)
-correct for
C_{2}
.
∎

We build now an execution covering
q_{f}
with
\Gamma^{\prime}
: let
C^{\prime}_{0}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{0})
defined by
L^{\prime}_{0}(v)=q_{in}
for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
. Obviously,

C^{\prime}_{0}
is
n
-correct. By
Lemma

23
, there exists a sequence of configurations
(C^{\prime}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
such that
for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
C^{\prime}_{i-1}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{i}
and
C^{\prime}_{i}
is
(n-i)
-correct for
C_{i}
. Hence,
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{n}
and
C^{\prime}_{n}
is
0
-correct for
C_{n}
and

L^{\prime}_{n}(\epsilon)=L_{n}(\lambda(\epsilon))=L_{n}(v_{f})=q_{f}
.

This allows to prove the following result.

Theorem 24
.

Cover
and
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
are equivalent.

Appendix B
Phase-bounded protocols as an under-approximation

Phase-bounded protocols can be seen as a semantic restriction of general protocols in which each process can only switch a bounded number of times between phases where it receives messages and phases where it can send messages. When, usually, restricting the behavior of processes immediately yields an
underapproximation of the reachable states, we highlight here the fact that preventing messages from being received can in fact lead to new reachable states.
This motivates our definition of phase-bounded protocols, in which a process always ends in a reception phase.

q_{in}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
q_{4}
q_{5}
q_{6}
p
!!c
?c
?m
!!m
!!b
?m
?a
?m
!!a

(a)

An example of a broadcast protocol
\overline{P}

q_{in}
(q_{1},b,1)
(q_{2},b,1)
(q_{3},r,2)
(q_{4},r,1)
(q_{5},b,2)
(q_{6},b,2)
(p,r,1)
!!c
?c
?m
!!m
!!b
?m
?a
!!a

(b)

Unfolding of
\overline{P}
limited to 2 phases:
(q,b,i)
means that the state
q
is reached in a broadcast phase when
(q,r,i)
means that it is reached in a reception phase. The number of the phase is given by
i
.

Figure 13
:
A protocol and its bounded unfolding showing that it may not be an underapproximation.

Indeed, consider the protocol pictured on
Figure

13
. The state
q_{3}
is not coverable: to cover
q_{3}
, a node
v_{1}
needs to receive message
a
when it is on state
q_{2}
from one of its neighbor
v_{2}
. By construction,
v_{1}
broadcasts message
m
before reaching
q_{2}
.
Vertex
v_{2}
can only broadcast message
a
when it is on state
q_{5}
. To reach
q_{5}
, vertex
v_{2}
visited exactly states
q_{in},q_{4}
and
q_{5}
. From each of those states, there is an outgoing reception transition labelled with
m
going to
p
. Hence, at any moment of the execution, the broadcast of message
m
by vertex
v_{1}
would have brought
v_{2}
in
p
, preventing the broadcast of
a
.
However, a naive 2-phase bounded unfolding of this protocol, in which we limit the number of phases of sending and reception to 2, is illustrated in
Figure

13
. In
this protocol,
(q_{3},r,2)
(hence
q_{3}
) is coverable:
consider
\Gamma=(\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\})
and the following execution:

\displaystyle(\Gamma,\{v_{1}\mapsto q_{in},v_{2}\mapsto q_{in}\})\rightarrow(\Gamma,\{v_{1}\mapsto(q_{1},b,1),v_{2}\mapsto(q_{4},r,1)\})\rightarrow(\Gamma,\{v_{1}\mapsto(q_{1},b,1),

\displaystyle v_{2}\mapsto(q_{5},b,2)\})\rightarrow(\Gamma,\{v_{1}\mapsto(q_{2},b,1),v_{2}\mapsto(q_{5},b,2)\})\rightarrow(\Gamma,\{v_{1}\mapsto(q_{3},r,2),v_{2}\mapsto(q_{6},b,2)\}).

In fact, in state
(q_{5},b,2)
a process is not allowed to switch anymore, hence the transition allowing to receive message
m
has been removed. Doing so,
we have made state
q_{3}
coverable. This shows that this type of bounded semantics does not give an underapproximation of the coverable states, in spite of what was expected.

Actually, the reception of a message is something that is not under the control of a process. If another process broadcasts a message, a faithful behavior of the system is that all the processes that can receive it indeed do so, no matter in which phase they are in their own execution. Hence, in a restriction that attempts to limit the number of switches
between sending and receiving phases, one should not prevent a reception to happen. This motivates our definition of a phase-bounded protocol.

Let
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
be a broadcast protocol, and
k\in{\rm Nature}
.

We define the
k
-unfolding of
P
denoted by
P_{k}
as the following protocol:

P_{k}=(Q_{k},\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta_{k})
with
Q_{k}=\{q^{0}\mid q\in Q\}\cup\{q^{b,j},q^{r,j}\mid q\in Q,1\leq j\leq k\}
. To ease the notations we let
q^{r,0}=q^{0}
,
q^{b,0}=q^{0}=
for all
q^{0}\in Q_{0}
.

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}

\displaystyle=\{(q^{0},\tau,p^{0})\mid(q,\tau,p)\in\Delta\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q^{r,j},\alpha,p^{r,j})\mid 1\leq j\leq k\text{ and }(q,\alpha,p)\in\Delta\text{ and }\alpha\in\{\tau\}\cup?\Sigma\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q^{b,j},\alpha,p^{b,j})\mid 1\leq j\leq k\text{ and }(q,\alpha,p)\in\Delta\text{ and }\alpha\in\{\tau\}\cup!!\Sigma\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q^{r,j},!!m,p^{b,j+1})\mid 0\leq j<k\text{ and }(q,!!m,p)\in\Delta\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q^{b,j},?m,p^{r,j+1})\mid 0\leq j<k\text{ and }(q,?m,p)\in\Delta\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(q^{b,k},?m,p^{r,k})\mid(q,?m,p)\in\Delta\}.

The last case of the definition of
\Delta_{k}
implements the fact that we never prevent a reception from occurring, even in the last phase.

The following lemma establishes that this definition of unfolding can be used as an underapproximation for
Cover
.

Lemma 25
.

q_{f}
can be covered in
P
if and only if there exist
k\in{\rm Nature}
,
y\in\{r,b\}
and
0\leq j\leq k
such that
(q_{f},y,j)
can be covered in
P_{k}
.

Proof.

Left-to-right direction:

Assume that
q_{f}
can be covered in
P
, and let
C_{0}\rightarrow C_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{n}
be an execution of
P
such that
C_{i}=(\Gamma,L_{i})
for all
0\leq i\leq n
and there exists
v_{f}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
such that
L_{n}(v_{f})=q_{f}
.
For all
v\in V
, we define
\kappa_{v}
a function associating to each
0\leq i \leq n
the number and the type of phase in which vertex
v
is. More formally,
\kappa_{v}(0)=0
and for
0\leq i<n
,

\kappa_{v}(i+1)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(b,1)&\mbox{if }\kappa_{v}(i)=0\text{ and }C_{i}\xrightarrow{v,t}C_{i+1}\text{ for some }t=(q,!!m,q^{\prime})\in\Delta,\\
&q,q^{\prime}\in Q,m\in\Sigma\\
(r,1)&\mbox{if }\kappa_{v}(i)=0\text{ and }C_{i}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime},t}C_{i+1}\text{ with }v\in\textsf{N}(v^{\prime})\text{ and }\\
&t=(q,!!m,q^{\prime})\in\Delta,q,q^{\prime}\in Q,m\in\Sigma\text{ and }(L_{i}(v),?m,L_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta\\
(b,j)&\mbox{if }\kappa_{v}(i)=(r,j-1)\text{ and }C_{i}\xrightarrow{v,t}C_{i+1}\text{ for some }t=(q,!!m,q^{\prime})\in\Delta,\\
&q,q^{\prime}\in Q,m\in\Sigma\\
(r,j)&\mbox{if }\kappa_{v}(i)=(b,j-1)\text{ and }C_{i}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime},t}C_{i+1}\text{ with }v\in\textsf{N}(v^{\prime})\text{ and }\\
&t=(q,!!m,q^{\prime})\in\Delta,q,q^{\prime}\in Q,m\in\Sigma\text{ and }(L_{i}(v),?m,L_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta\\
\kappa_{v}(i)&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

We define
k=\max_{v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)}\{x_{v}\mid\kappa_{v}(n)=(x_{v},y),y\in\{r,b\}\}
. Since, for all
0\leq i<n
, for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
,

\kappa_{v}(i)\leq\kappa_{v}(i+1)
, it holds that
k\geq\{x_{v}\mid\kappa_{v}(i)=(x_{v},y),y\in\{r,b\},0\leq i\leq n\}
. We now consider
P_{k}=(Q_{k},\Sigma,q_{in}^{0},\Delta_{k})
the
k
-unfolding of
P
.
For each
0\leq i\leq n
, for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, if
L_{i}(v)=q
we let
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=q^{\kappa_{v}(i)}
. We now show
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{n}
is an execution of
P_{k}
where for all
0\leq i\leq n
,
C^{\prime}_{i}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime}_{i})
.
Consider
C^{\prime}_{0}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime}_{0})
with
L^{\prime}_{0}(v)=q_{in}^{0}
for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
. As
\kappa_{v}(0)=0
for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, the induction property holds and
C^{\prime}_{0}
is initial.
Assume we proved that
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{i}
. We have to prove that
C^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{i+1}
.
Denote
v_{!}
the vertex such that
C_{i}\xrightarrow{v_{!},t}C_{i+1}
.

If
t=(q_{1},\tau,q_{2})
for some
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q
, for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
,
\kappa_{v}(i+1)=\kappa_{v}(i)
and if
v\neq v_{!}
,
L_{i+1}(v)=L_{i}(v)
hence,
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L^{\prime}_{i}(v)
. By definition of
P_{k}
,
(q_{1}^{\kappa_{v_{!}}(i)},\tau,q_{2}^{\kappa_{v_{!}}(i+1)})\in\Delta_{k}
. Hence, if
\alpha=\tau
,
C^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

Now let
t=(q_{1},!!m,q_{2})
for some
m\in\Sigma
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q
. We start by observing that for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus(\{v_{!}\}\cup\textsf{N}(v_{!}))
,
\kappa_{v}(i+1)=\kappa_{v}(i)
and
L_{i+1}(v)=L_{i}(v)
hence,
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L^{\prime}_{i}(v)
.

Observe that, either (a)
\kappa_{v_{!}}(i+1)=(b,j)
and
\kappa_{v_{!}}(i)=(r,j-1)
(or
\kappa_{v}(i)=0
) for some
j\leq k
, or (b)
\kappa_{v_{!}}(i+1)=\kappa_{v_{!}}(i)
.
In case (a),
L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{!})=q_{1}^{(r,j-1)}
or
L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{!})=q_{1}^{0}
, and
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v_{!})=L_{i+1}(v_{!})^{\kappa_{v_{!}}(i+1)}=L_{i+1}(v_{!})^{(b,j)}=q_{2}^{(b,j)}
. By definition,
(q_{1}^{(r,j-1)},!!m,q_{2}^{(b,j)})\in\Delta_{k}
(or
(q_{1}^{0},!!m,q_{2}^{b,1})\in\Delta_{k}
).

In case (b),
\kappa_{v}(i)=\kappa_{v}(i+1)=(b,j)
for some
1\leq j\leq k
. Then,
L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{!})=q_{1}^{(b,j)}
and
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v_{!})=L_{i+1}(v_{!})^{\kappa_{v_{!}}(i+1)}=L_{i+1}(v_{!})^{b,j}=q_{2}^{(b,j)}
. By definition,
(q_{1}^{(b,j)},!!m,q_{2}^{(b,j)})\in\Delta_{k}
.

Let now
v\in\textsf{N}(v_{!})
. If there is no
q^{\prime}\in Q
such that
(L_{i}(v),?m,L_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta
, then
L_{i}(v)=L_{i+1}(v)
and
\kappa_{v}(i+1)=\kappa_{v}(i)
. Hence
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L_{i+1}(v)^{\kappa_{v}(i+1)}=L_{i}(v)^{\kappa_{v}(i)}=L^{\prime}_{i}(v)
. Furthermore, by construction of
P_{k}
, there is no transition
(L^{\prime}_{i}(v),?m,p^{\kappa})\in\Delta_{k}
for some
p^{\kappa}\in Q_{k}
as otherwise, there would be a transition
(L_{i}(v),?m,p)\in\Delta
, which contradicts the fact that
L_{i}(v)=L_{i+1}(v)
.

Let now
v\in\textsf{N}(v_{!})
such that
(L_{i}(v),?m,L_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta
.
By definition of
\kappa_{v}
, again, either (a)
\kappa_{v}(i+1)=(r,j)
and
\kappa_{v}(i)=(b,j-1)
(or
\kappa_{v}(i)=0
) for some
1\leq j
, or (b)
\kappa_{v}(i+1)=\kappa_{v}(i)
. In that case,
\kappa_{v}(i)=(r,j)
for some
j\geq 1
. In both cases, by construction of
k
, it holds that
j\leq k
.
Hence, by definition of
\Delta_{k}
,
in case (a),
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=L_{i}(v)^{(b,j-1)}
and
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L_{i+1}(v)^{(r,j)}
and
(L^{\prime}_{i}(v),?m,L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta_{k}
, and in case (b),

L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=L_{i}(v)^{(r,j)}
and
L_{i+1}(v)=L_{i+1}(v)^{(r,j)}
. Hence,
(L^{\prime}_{i}(v),?m,L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta_{k}
.

We conclude that
C^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

Hence,
L^{\prime}_{n}(v_{f})=q_{f}^{\kappa_{v_{f}}(n)}
and so there exists
0\leq j\leq k
and
y\in\{r,b\}
such that
q_{f}^{y,j}
is coverable in
P_{k}
.

Right-to-left direction:
Conversely, assume that there exist
k\in{\rm Nature}
,
0\leq j\leq k
and
y\in\{r,b\}
such that
q_{f}^{y,j}
is coverable in
P_{k}
.
Let
C_{0}\rightarrow C_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{n}
an execution such that
C_{n}=(\Gamma,L_{n})
and there exists
v_{f}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
with
L_{n}(v_{f})=q_{f}^{y,j}
. We show that there exists an execution
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{n}
such that for all
1\leq i\leq n
, for
C^{\prime}_{i}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime}_{i})
and any vertex
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
,
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=q
if and only if
there exists
\kappa_{v,i}\in\{0\}\cup(\{r,b\}\times[0,n])
such that
L_{i}(v)=q^{\kappa_{v,i}}
.

Let
C^{\prime}_{0}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime}_{0})
with
L_{0}(v)=q_{in}
for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
. As
L^{\prime}_{0}(v)=q_{in}^{0}
for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, the induction hypothesis holds.
Assume we proved that
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{i}
where for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
,
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=q
if and only if
there exists
\kappa_{v,i}\in\{0\}\cup(\{r,b\}\times[0,n])
such that
L_{i}(v)=q^{\kappa_{v,i}}
.

Let
C^{\prime}_{i+1}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime}_{i+1})
be the configuration such that for all
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
,
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=q
if and only if there exists
\kappa_{v,i+1}\in\{0\}\cup(\{r,b\}\times[0,n])
such that
L_{i+1}(v)=q^{\kappa_{v,i+1}}
. We prove now that
C^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

Denote
C_{i}\xrightarrow{v_{!},t}C_{i+1}
and
t=(q_{1}^{\kappa_{v_{!},i}},\alpha,q_{2}^{\kappa_{v_{!},i+1}})\in\Delta_{k}
. From the definition of
\Delta_{k}
, it holds that
(q_{1},\alpha,q_{2})\in\Delta
.
Hence, if
\alpha=\tau
,
C^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{i+1}
, as for all other nodes,
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=q
if and only if there exists
\kappa_{v,i+1}\in\{0\}\cup(\{r,b\}\times[0,n])
such that
L_{i+1}(v)=q^{\kappa_{v,i+1}}
. Furthermore,
L_{i+1}(v)=L_{i}(v)=q^{\kappa_{v,i+1}}
hence
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=q=L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)
.

Assume now that
\alpha=!!m
for some
m\in\Sigma
and let
v\in\textsf{N}(v_{!})
. Either
(a)
(L_{i}(v),?m,L_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta_{k}
, or (b) there is no
p\in Q_{k}
such that
(L_{i}(v),?m,p)\in\Delta_{k}
. In case (a), by construction of
\Delta_{k}
, there exists
(p_{1},?m,p_{2})\in\Delta
such that
L_{i}(v)=p_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}
and
L_{i+1}(v)=p_{2}^{\kappa_{2}}
for some
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\{0\}\cup(\{r,b\}\times[1,n])
Hence,
(L^{\prime}_{i}(v),?m,L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v))\in\Delta
.

In case (b), we show that there is no
p\in Q
such that
(L^{\prime}_{i}(v),?m,p)\in\Delta
. Assume by contradiction that this is the case, and denote
L_{i}(v)=p_{1}^{\kappa}
and
(p_{1},?m,p_{2})\in\Delta
. If
\kappa=0
, then by definition of
\Delta_{k}
,
(p_{1}^{\kappa},?m,p_{2}^{r,1})\in\Delta_{k}
and we reach a contradiction.
If
\kappa=(r,j)
for some
1\leq j\leq k
, by definition of
\Delta_{k}
,
(p_{1}^{(r,j)},?m,p_{2}^{(r,j)})\in\Delta_{k}
and again, we reach a contradiction.
If
\kappa=(b,j)
, by definition of
\Delta_{k}
:
(p_{1}^{(b,j)},?m,p_{2}^{(r,j+1)})\in\Delta_{k}
and we reach a contradiction.
Finally, if
\kappa=(b,k)
then,
by definition of
\Delta_{k}
:
(p_{1}^{(b,k)},?m,p_{2}^{(r,k)})\in\Delta_{k}
and we reached a contradiction.

Hence, there is no transition
(p_{1},?m,p_{2})\in\Delta
for some
p_{2}\in Q
.

We conclude that
C^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{i+1}
. Hence, there is an execution
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{n}
of
P
with
L_{n}(v_{f})=q_{f}
and
q_{f}
is coverable in
P
.
∎

Appendix C
Undecidability proof of
Cover
(
Section

4
)

We reduce the coverability problem of a Minsky machine to the
Cover
problem in broadcast networks with 6-phase-bounded protocols.

Minsky Machines.

A Minsky Machine
M
is a tuple
M=(\text{Loc},\text{Trans},\ell_{0},\ell_{f},\textsf{x}_{1},\textsf{x}_{2})
such that
Loc
is a finite state of locations,
\ell_{in}\in\text{Loc}
,
\ell_{f}\in\text{Loc}
,
\textsf{x}_{1},\textsf{x}_{2}
are two counters
and
Trans
is a finite set of transitions such that
\text{Trans}\subseteq\text{Loc}\times\{\text{inc(}\textsf{x}_{i}\text{)},\text{dec(}\textsf{x}_{i}\text{)},\text{test(}\textsf{x}_{i}\text{)}\mid i=1,2\}\times\text{Loc}
.
We denote a configuration of
M
by a tuple
(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})
where
\ell\in\text{Loc}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in{\rm Nature}
are values of the two counters (respectively
\textsf{x}_{1}
and
\textsf{x}_{2}
).
Let
(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})
and
(\ell^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})
be two configurations, and
t\in\text{Trans}
. We note
(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})\xrightarrow{t}(\ell^{\prime},x_{1},x_{2})
with
t=(\ell,\text{op},\ell^{\prime})
and one of the following conditions holds:

•

\text{op}=\text{inc(}\textsf{x}_{i}\text{)}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}+1
and
x^{\prime}_{3-i}=x_{3-i}
;

•

\text{op}=\text{dec(}\textsf{x}_{i}\text{)}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}-1
and
x^{\prime}_{3-i}=x_{3-i}
;

•

\text{op}=\text{test(}\textsf{x}_{i}\text{)}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}=0
and
x^{\prime}_{3-i}=x_{3-i}
.

The halting problem asks if there is a sequence of transitions
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
such that
(\ell_{in},0,0)\xrightarrow{t_{1}}(\ell_{1},x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1})\xrightarrow{t_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{t_{n}}(\ell_{f},x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n})
for
x_{1}^{i},x_{2}^{i}\in{\rm Nature}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
x_{1}^{n}=x_{2}^{n}=0
. It is known to be undecidable. We denote
X
for the set of counters
\{\textsf{x}_{1},\textsf{x}_{2}\}
.

C.1
Definition of
P
.

Let
M=(\text{Loc},\text{Trans},\ell_{in},\ell_{f},\textsf{x}_{1},\textsf{x}_{2})
be a Minsky Machine.
The protocol
P
of the reduction is described in
Figure

14
and is defined on a finite alphabet
\Sigma
that we define now. We start by defining
\text{OP}=\{\text{test}_{\textsf{x}},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1},\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}},\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}}\mid\textsf{x}\in X\}
. Next, for
0\leq i\leq 2
, we define
\text{OP}^{i}=\{\textsf{op}^{i}\mid\textsf{op}\in\text{OP}\}
and

\text{OK}^{i}=\{\text{done}_{\textsf{op}}^{i}\mid\textsf{op}\in\text{OP}\}
.
We can now define the alphabet
\Sigma=\{\text{done, 0, 1, 2}\}\cup\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq 2}\text{OP}^{i}\cup\text{OK}^{i}
. We also define for
0\leq i\leq 2
two operations:
i\ominus 1=(i-1)\mod 3
and
i\oplus 1=(i+1)\mod 3
.

A transition from a sub protocol (an orange box) to a state means that from any states of the sub protocol, there is an outgoing transition with the same label and to the same state. Hence, any state
q
of
P_{M}
has outgoing transitions
(q,?1,\frownie)
,

(q,?2,\frownie)
and
(q,?0,\frownie)
.

The main idea is to build a protocol that will ensure, during an initialization phase, a specific organization among processes depicted in
Figure

15
.
In particular, after the initialization phase, processes will be organized as line
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
with the first process
v_{0}
in a state
\ell_{in}
corresponding to the initial location of the machine, the last process
v_{n}
in a state
q^{\textsf{tail}}
, and for
1\leq i<n
, process
v_{i}
is on state
\textsf{z}^{b_{i}}
where
b_{i}=i\mod 3
. This way, a process
v_{i}
(for some
0\leq i<n
) can
distinguish
between its two neighbors (intuitively between its right and left). For instance, if
n<5
, process
v_{4}
is on a state
\textsf{z}^{1}
and has its left neighbor
v_{3}
on
\textsf{z}^{0}
and its right neighbor
v_{5}
on a state
\textsf{z}^{2}
.

P_{M}
,
P_{\textsf{tail}}
and
P_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq 2
will be defined on the same alphabet of messages
\Sigma
.

•

We describe
P_{M}=(Q^{M},\Delta^{M})
in
Figure

16
, this is where the machine is simulated: the process
v_{0}
evolves in the sets
Loc
, we will see that if it takes a transition of the machine which should not be taken (a false test to 0, a decrement not possible, or an increment not feasible because of lack of processes), then other processes will fail in their execution, and
q_{f}
will not be covered.

•

We describe
P_{1}=(Q^{1},\Delta^{1})
in
Figure

18
. The processes evolving on
P_{1}
will simulate counters’ values, each of them representing one unit of one of the counter: when the process is on
\textsf{x}_{1}^{1}
[resp.
\textsf{x}_{2}^{1}
] it represents a unit of counter
\textsf{x}_{1}
[resp.
\textsf{x}_{2}
].

•

P_{2}
and
P_{3}
are obtained from
P_{1}
by replacing each
j\in\{0,1,2\}
appearing in the protocol (states and transitions) by
j\oplus 1
for
P_{2}
and
j\ominus 1
for
P_{0}
. As in
P_{1}
, processes evolving on
P_{2}
or
P_{0}
will simulate counters’ values: for
\textsf{x} \in X
, a process on
\textsf{x}^{2}
or
\textsf{x}^{0}
represents one unit of
x
.

•

We describe
P_{\textsf{tail}}=(Q^{t},\Delta^{t})
in
Figure

17
. The process
v_{n}
will check that all the operations have been correctly executed. When it will receive message "
done
" (first sent by the process
P_{M}
), it will reach state
q_{f}
.
