Figure 18
:
Description of
P_{1}
. We draw two states
\textsf{z}^{1}
for readability’s sake.
P_{2}
is obtained by replacing each
j\in\{0,1,2\}
by
j\oplus 1
and
P_{0}
by replacing each
j\in\{0,1,2\}
by
j\ominus 1
.

State
\frownie
is a deadlock state (there is no outgoing transitions from
\frownie
). In order for
P
to be phase-bounded, we actually duplicate
\frownie
state as much as needed so there is no contradictions (one state per reception phase). For instance, if there are two receptions transitions
(q,?m,\frownie)
,
(q^{\prime},?m^{\prime},\frownie)
with
q
and
q^{\prime}
not in the same phase, we duplicate
\frownie
into two states
\frownie,\frownie^{\prime}
such that
(q,?m,\frownie)
, and
(q^{\prime},?m^{\prime},\frownie^{\prime})
.
For ease of notation, when referring to any of those deadlock states, we use
\frownie
.

As a result, we get the following lemma.

Lemma 26
.

P
is 6-phase-bounded.

Proof.

We give the partition of states satisfying the 6-phase-bounded protocols condition.

•

Q_{0}=\{q_{in}\}
;

•

Q_{1}^{b}=\{q_{1}\}
and
Q_{1}^{r}=\{q_{1}^{i}\mid i\in\{0,1,2\}\}\cup\{q_{1}^{\textsf{tail}}\}\cup\{\frownie\}
;

•

Q_{2}^{b}=\{q_{2}^{i}\mid i\in\{0,1,2\}\}\cup\{q_{2}^{\textsf{tail}}\}
and
Q_{2}^{r}=\{q_{2}\}\cup\{\frownie\}
;

•

Q_{3}^{b}=\{\ell,q_{t},q_{f}^{M}\mid\ell\in\text{Loc},t\in\text{Trans}\}
and
Q_{3}^{r}=\{q_{3}^{i}\mid i\in\{0,1,2\}\}\cup Q^{t}\cup\{\frownie\}
;

•

Q_{4}^{b}=\{q\in Q^{i}\setminus{\frownie,\text{d}^{i},\text{d}^{\prime i}}i\in\{0,1,2\}\}
and
Q_{4}^{r}=\{\frownie\}
;

•

Q_{5}^{b}=\emptyset
and
Q_{5}^{r}=\{\text{d}^{i}\mid i\in\{0,1,2\}\}\cup\{\frownie\}
;

•

Q_{6}^{b}=\{\text{d}^{\prime i}\mid i\in\{0,1,2\}\}
and
Q_{6}^{r}=\{\frownie\}
.

∎

Note that
P
is also
k
-phase-bounded for any
k>6
by taking
Q_{7}^{r}=\{\frownie\}
,
Q_{7}^{b}=\emptyset
and
Q_{j}^{y}=\emptyset
for any
j\geq 8
and
y\in\{r,b\}
.

In the rest of this section, we denote
b_{i}=i\mod 3
for all
i\in{\rm Nature}
.

C.2
Completeness of the Reduction.

Lemma 27
.

If there is a transition sequence
t_{1},\dots t_{n}
such that
(\ell_{in},0,0)\xrightarrow{t_{1}}(\ell_{1},x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1})\xrightarrow{t_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{t_{n}}(\ell_{f},0,0)
, then there exist
C\in\mathcal{I},C^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
, such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}
and there exists
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
such that
L^{\prime}(v)=q_{f}
.

Proof.

We denote by
m^{\prime}
the maximal value reached by counters during the machine execution, i.e.
m^{\prime}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}(x_{1}^{i}+x_{2}^{i})
.
We define
m
as
m^{\prime}+1
.
We suppose wlog that
m\mod 3=1
, if it is not the case, we redefine
m
as the first integer greater than
m
equal to 1 modulo 3.

Define
\Gamma=(V,E)
such that:
V=\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m},v_{m+1}\}
and
E=\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid 0\leq i<m+1\}
, and take
C_{0}=(\Gamma,L_{0})
where for all
v\in V
,
L_{0}(v)=q_{in}
.

We define the following sequence of configurations:

C_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{0},t_{0}}C_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},t_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{v_{m+1},t_{m+1}}C_{m+2}\xrightarrow{v_{0},t^{\prime}_{0}}C_{m+2}\xrightarrow{v_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}}C_{2m+3}
where:

t_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(q_{in},!!0,q_{1})&\mbox{for }i=0\\
(q_{1}^{b_{i}},!!b_{i},q_{2}^{b_{i}})&\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq m\\
(q_{1}^{\textsf{tail}},!!2,q^{\textsf{tail}})&\mbox{for }i=m+1.\end{array}\right.

and

t^{\prime}_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(q_{2},!!\$,\ell_{in})&\mbox{for }i=0\\
(q_{3}^{b_{i}},!!\$,\textsf{z}^{b_{i}})&\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq m.\end{array}\right.

Denote
C_{m+2}=(\Gamma,L_{m+2})
and
C_{2m+3}=(\Gamma,L_{2m+3})
, it holds that:
L_{m+2}(v_{0})=q_{2}
, and
L_{m+2}(v_{i})=q_{3}^{b_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
L_{m+2}(v_{m+1})=q_{2}^{\textsf{tail}}
.
And:
L_{2m+3}(v_{0})=\ell_{in}
,
L_{2m+3}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
and for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
L_{2m+3}(v_{i})=\textsf{z}^{b_{i}}
.

We will now build
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow^{+}C^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow^{+}\dots\rightarrow^{+}C^{\prime}_{n}
such that for all
0\leq i\leq n
,
C^{\prime}_{i}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime}_{i})
, and:
L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{0})=\ell_{i}
,
L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
, and
x_{1}^{i}=|\{v\mid L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=\textsf{x}_{1}^{j},0\leq j\leq 2\}|
,
x_{2}^{i}=|\{v\mid L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=\textsf{x}_{2}^{j},0\leq j\leq 2\}|
, and for all
1\leq j\leq m
,
L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{j})\in\{\mathsf{z}^{b_{j}},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{j}},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{j}}\}
.

For
i=0
, we take
C^{\prime}_{0}=C_{2m+3}
. By construction, it respects the conditions.
Assume we built
C^{\prime}_{i}
for some
0\leq i<n
, and consider
(\ell_{i},x_{1}^{i},x_{2}^{i})\xrightarrow{t_{i+1}}(\ell_{i+1},x_{1}^{i+1},x_{2}^{i+1})
. For readability’s sake, we note
t=(\ell,\text{op},\ell^{\prime})
and we rename
x_{1}^{i},x_{2}^{i},x_{1}^{i+1},x_{2}^{i+1}
by
x_{1},x_{2},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}
, finally we rename
C^{\prime}_{i}
by
C
.

By induction hypothesis,
C=(\Gamma,L)
is such that
L(v_{0})=\ell
,
L(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
and
x_{1}=|\{v\mid L(v)=\textsf{x}_{1}^{j},0\leq j\leq 2\}|
,
x_{2}=|\{v\mid L(v)=\textsf{x}_{2}^{j},0\leq j\leq 2\}|
, and for all
1\leq j\leq m
,
L(v_{j})\in\{\mathsf{z}^{k},\textsf{x}_{1}^{k},\textsf{x}_{2}^{k}\mid k=j\mod 3\}
.
We distinguish the following cases:

•

\text{op}=\text{inc(}\textsf{x}\text{)}
with
\textsf{x}\in X
, then by definition of
m
,
x_{1}+x_{2}<m-1
.
Hence,
|\{v\mid L(v)=\textsf{x}_{1}^{j},0\leq j\leq 2\}|+|\{v\mid L(v)=\textsf{x}_{2}^{j},0\leq j\leq 2\}|<m-1
, and so there exists
v_{f}\in\{v_{1},\dots,v_{m-1}\}
such that
L(v_{f})\nin\{\textsf{x}_{2}^{j},\textsf{x}_{1}^{j}\mid 0\leq j\leq 2\}
with
1\leq f\leq m
. Let us denote
f
the first such index. By hypothesis,
L(v_{f})\in\{\mathsf{z}^{k},\textsf{x}_{1}^{k},\textsf{x}_{2}^{k}\}
for
k=f\mod 3
, hence
L(v_{f})=\mathsf{z}^{k}
.

We denote
y
the counter such that
\{\textsf{x},\textsf{y}\}=\{\textsf{x}_{1},\textsf{x}_{2}\}
.
We also denote
p_{i}:=L(v_{i})
, and
p^{\prime}_{i}
the state such that:

p^{\prime}_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r^{b_{i}}_{\text{inc}_{\textsf{x}}}&\mbox{if }p_{i}=\mathsf{z}^{b_{i}},i\neq f\\
r^{b_{i},\textsf{x}}_{\text{inc}_{\textsf{x}}}&\mbox{if }p_{i}=\textsf{x}^{b_{i}},i\neq f\\
r^{b_{i}\textsf{x}}_{\text{inc}_{\textsf{x}}}&\mbox{if }p_{i}=\textsf{y}^{b_{i}},i\neq f\\
q_{\textsf{x}}^{b_{i}}&\mbox{if }i=f\end{array}\right.

Consider the following sequence:

\displaystyle C^{0}\xrightarrow{v_{0},(\ell,!!\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{0},q_{t})}C^{1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},(p_{1},!!\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{b_{1}},p^{\prime}_{1})}C^{2}\xrightarrow{v_{0},(q_{t},!!\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{0},\ell^{\prime})}C^{3}\xrightarrow{v_{2},(p_{2},!!\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{b_{2}},p^{\prime}_{2})}

\displaystyle C^{4}\xrightarrow{v_{1},(p^{\prime}_{1},!!\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{1}},p_{1})}C^{5}\xrightarrow{v_{3},(p_{3},!!\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{b_{3}},p^{\prime}_{3})}C^{6}\xrightarrow{v_{2},(p^{\prime}_{2},!!\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{2}},p_{2})}

\displaystyle C^{7}\xrightarrow{v_{4},(p_{4},!!\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{b_{4}},p^{\prime}_{4})}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{f-1},(p_{f-1},!!\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{b_{f-1}},p^{\prime}_{f-1})}C^{2(f-1)}\xrightarrow{v_{f-2},(p^{\prime}_{f-2},!!\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{f-2}},p_{f-2})}

\displaystyle C^{2f-1}\xrightarrow{v_{f},(p_{f},!!\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{b_{f}},p^{\prime}_{f})}C^{2f}\xrightarrow{v_{f-1},(p^{\prime}_{f-1},!!\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{f-1}},p_{f-1})}

\displaystyle C^{2f+1}\xrightarrow{v_{f+1},(p_{f+1},!!{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{f+1}},p^{\prime}_{f+1})}C^{2f+2}\xrightarrow{v_{f},(p^{\prime}_{f},!!\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{f}},\textsf{x}^{b_{f}})}

\displaystyle C^{2f+3}\xrightarrow{v_{f+2},(p_{f+2},!!{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{f+2}},p^{\prime}_{b_{f+2}})}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{m},(p_{m},!!\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{m}},p^{\prime}_{m})}

\displaystyle C^{2m}\xrightarrow{v_{m-1},(p^{\prime}_{m-1},!!\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{m-1}},p_{m-1})}C^{2m+1}\xrightarrow{v_{m},(p^{\prime}_{m},!!\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{m}},p_{b_{m}})}C^{2m+2}

Note that if
f=1
,
v_{1}
broadcasts
{\text{inc}}_{\textsf{x}}^{b_{1}}
and
\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{1}}
, and every vertices except
v_{0}
broadcast overlined messages as well. Furthermore, as
f\leq m-1
,
v_{m}
always broadcasts
\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{m}}\cdot\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b_{m}}
, hence
L^{2m}(v_{m+1})=q_{\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}}^{t}
and
L^{2m+2}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
.
At the end of the sequence,
C^{2m+2}=(\Gamma,L^{2m+2})
is such that
L^{2m+2}(v_{0})=\ell^{\prime}
,
L^{2m+2}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
, and,
L^{2m+2}(v_{f})=\textsf{x}^{b_{f}}
and for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
i\neq f
,
L^{2m+2}(v_{i})=L(v_{i})
.

•

\text{op}=\text{dec(}\textsf{x}\text{)}
, the sequence is analogous, this time we justify the existence of one process on a state
\textsf{x}^{j}
for some
0\leq j\leq 2
by induction hypothesis.

•

\text{op}=\text{test(}\textsf{x}\text{)}
, the only difference is that the propagated message does not change, all processes can propagate the message as no processes is on a state
\textsf{x}^{i}
for some
0\leq i\leq 2
(this is true by induction hypothesis).

Hence, we built a sequence of configurations
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow^{+}C^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow^{+}\cdots\rightarrow^{+}C^{\prime}_{n}
such that
C^{\prime}_{n}=(\Gamma,L_{n})
with
L_{n}(v_{0})=\ell_{f}
,
L_{n}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
and for all
0\leq i\leq m
,
L_{n}(v_{i})=\mathsf{z}^{b_{i}}
. Finally, we build the sequence leading to the final configuration, from
C^{\prime}_{n}
:

C^{\prime}_{n}\xrightarrow{v_{0},(\ell_{f},!!\text{done},q_{f}^{M})}C^{\prime}_{n+1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},(d^{b_{1}},!!\text{done},d^{{}^{\prime}b_{1}})}C^{\prime}_{n+2}\xrightarrow{v_{2},(d^{b_{2}},!!\text{done},d^{{}^{\prime}b_{2}})}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{m},(d^{b_{m}},!!\text{done},d^{{}^{\prime}b_{m}})}C^{\prime}_{n+m+1}
. Denote
C^{\prime}_{n+m+1}=(\Gamma,L_{n+m+1})
, it holds that
L_{n+m+1}(v_{m+1})=q_{f}
.
∎

C.3
Correctness of the Reduction.

This subsection is devoted to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 28
.

If there exist
C\in\mathcal{I}
,
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}
and there exists
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
such that
L^{\prime}(v)=q_{f}
, then there exists a transition sequence
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
such that
(\ell_{in},0,0)\xrightarrow{t_{1}}(\ell_{1},x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1})\xrightarrow{t_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{t_{n}}(\ell_{f},0,0)
.

We say that a process
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
is active if it broadcasts (at least) one message during the execution.
We denote
C_{0}\rightarrow C_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{n}
where for each
0\leq i\leq n
,
C_{i}=(\Gamma,L_{i})
,
C_{0}=C
and
C_{n}=C^{\prime}
. Furthermore, we denote
v_{f}
the process covering
q_{f}
.
Lastly, for sake of readability, for
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
,
m\in\Sigma
and
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, we denote
C\xrightarrow{v,a}C^{\prime}
when there exists a transition
(q,!!a,q^{\prime})\in\Delta
for some
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
such that
C\xrightarrow{v,(q,!!a,q^{\prime})}C^{\prime}
.

C.3.1
First step: Structure.

In a first step we prove that from the execution between
C
and
C^{\prime}
, one can extract a set of vertices
V=\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m},v_{m+1}\}
for some
m\in{\rm Nature}
forming a
line
(i.e. for all
0\leq i\leq m
,
\langle v_{i},v_{i+1}\rangle\in\textsf{E}(\Gamma)
). And such that
v_{m+1}
covers
q_{f}
and neighbors of vertices in
V
which are not in
V
are not active (i.e. they do not broadcast anything).
This subsubsection is devoted to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 29
.

There exists
V=\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m},v_{m+1}\}\subseteq\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, such that for all
0\leq i\leq m
,
\langle v_{i},v_{i+1}\rangle\in\textsf{E}(\Gamma)
and
\exists j_{0},j_{1},\dots,j_{m}
such that
L_{j_{0}}(v_{0})=\ell_{in}
,
L_{j_{m}}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
and for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
L_{j_{i}}(v_{i})=\textsf{z}^{b_{i}}
and for all other vertex
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus V
which is a neighbor of a vertex
v^{\prime}\in V
,
v
is not active. Furthermore,
m\mod 3=1
.

By construction of the protocol,
v_{f}
receives message "
done
" from a process
v_{1}
before reaching
q_{f}
. This process again either sends "
done
" from state
\ell_{f}
or receives it before broadcasting it from a new process
v_{2}
(note that
v_{2}\neq v_{f}
as "
done
" can be sent only once and
v_{f}
broadcasts it after the broadcast of
v_{1}
). In the second case, we can reiterate the reasoning. We do so until finding a process (let us denote it
v_{m+1}
) sending "
done
" from
\ell_{f}
and we note
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
the intermediate processes. The process
v_{m+1}
exists as there is a finite number of processes. Furthermore,
\langle v_{f},v_{1}\rangle\in\textsf{E}(\Gamma)
and for all
0\leq i<m
,
\langle v_{i},v_{i+1}\rangle\in\textsf{E}(\Gamma)
. We rename
v_{i}
by
v_{m+1-i}
for all
1\leq i\leq m+1
, and
v_{f}
by
v_{m+1}
. Denote
V=\{v_{0},\dots,v_{m+1}\}
.
Note that there exists
j_{0}<j_{m+1}
such that
L_{j_{0}}(v_{0})=\ell_{in}
as
v_{0}
broadcasts "done" from
\ell_{f}
, and
L_{j_{m+1}}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
as
v_{m+1}
reaches state
q_{f}
from
q^{\textsf{tail}}
after
v_{0}
reached
\ell_{f}
.
Furthermore, observe that for all
v\in V\setminus\{v_{0},v_{m+1}\}
, there exists
i\in\{0,1,2\}
such that there exists
j_{1}<j
with
L_{j}(v)=\textsf{z}^{i}
.

We get the two following lemmas.

Lemma 30
.

v_{0}
has only one active neighbor before broadcasting "done".

Lemma 31
.

v_{m+1}
has only one active neighbor before receiving "done".

Indeed, otherwise,
v_{0}
(or
v_{m}
) has two neighbors sending two messages
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{0,1,2\}
, by construction of
P
, one of the message will bring
v_{0}
in
\frownie
.
With a similar argument, we get the following lemma.

Lemma 32
.

Let
v_{k}\in V
for some
1\leq k\leq m
,
v_{k}
has only two active neighbors before broadcasting "done".

We can now prove the following lemma.

Lemma 33
.

There exist
j_{0}<j_{1}<\cdots<j_{m}
,
such that for all
0\leq k\leq m+1
,
C_{j_{k}}\xrightarrow{v_{k},b_{k}}C_{j_{k}+1}
.
Furthermore
m+1=2\mod 3
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on
0\leq k\leq m+1
.
For
k=0
, it follows directly from the construction of
P
and the fact that there exists
j
such that
L_{j}(v_{0})=\ell_{f}
(
v_{0}
is the first process to broadcast "done"). Denote
j_{0}
such that
C_{j_{0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},0}C_{j_{0}+1}
.

Assume the property to hold for some
1\leq k\leq m+1
and denote
j_{0}<j_{1}<\cdots<j_{k}
such that for all
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
,
C_{j_{k^{\prime}}}\xrightarrow{v_{k^{\prime}},b_{k^{\prime}}}C_{j_{k^{\prime}}+1}
.

Case
k+1<m+1
.

As
k+1<m+1
, by construction of
V
, there exists
i\in\{0,1,2\}
such that there exists
j
with
L_{j}(v_{k+1})=\textsf{z}^{i}
.
By
Lemma

32
,
v_{k+1}
has exactly two active neighbors, which are by construction of
V
,
v_{k}
and
v_{k+2}
.
Denote
i_{k+1} \in\{0,1,2\}
the integer such that
v_{k+1}
receives "done" from
\textsf{z}^{i_{k+1}}
.
By induction hypothesis,
C_{j_{k}}\xrightarrow{v_{k},b_{k}}C_{j_{k}+1}
.
We distinguish two cases: (a)
k=0
and (b)
k>0
.

Subcase (a)
k=0
.

Assume
v_{k+1}
broadcasts
i_{k+1}
from an index
j<j_{0}
. Observe that
L_{j}(v_{0})=q_{in}
and so
L_{j+1}(v_{0})=q_{1}^{i_{k+1}}
which contradicts the fact that
v_{0}
broadcasts "done" from
\ell_{f}
, hence,
j_{0}<j
, and so
L_{j_{0}}(v_{k+1})=q_{in}
, and
L_{j_{0}+1}(v_{1})=q_{1}^{1}
. Hence
i_{k+1}=1
and
k+1=1
.

Subcase
k>0
.
Consider
v_{k-1}
, by induction hypothesis
j_{k-1} <j_{k}
with
C_{j_{k-1}}\xrightarrow{v_{k-1},b_{k-1}}C_{j_{k-1}+1}
and
C_{k}\xrightarrow{v_{k},b_{k}}C_{k+1}
. Hence, as by
Lemma

32
,
v_{k}
has only two active neighbors, only
v_{k+1}
can broadcast
(b_{k}+1)\mod 3
once
v_{k}
broadcast
b_{k}
. Hence,
i_{k+1}=(b_{k}+1)\mod 3=b_{k+1}
. Hence there exists
j_{k+1}>j_{k}
such that
C_{j_{k+1}}\xrightarrow{v_{k+1},b_{k+1}}C_{j_{k+1}+1}
.

Case
k+1=m+1
.
By construction of
V
, there exists
j^{\prime}_{m+1}
such that
L_{j^{\prime}_{m+1}}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
. By
Lemma

31
,
v_{m+1}
has exactly one active neighbor and this neighbor broadcasts "1". As
m\geq 1
, consider
v_{m}
. By induction hypothesis, and construction of
P
, there exists a unique
j_{m}
such that
C_{j_{m}}\xrightarrow{v_{m},b_{m}}C_{j_{m}+1}
. Hence,
b_{m}=m\mod 3=1
, finally there exists
j_{m+1}>j_{m}
such that
C_{j_{m+1}}\xrightarrow{v_{m+1},2}C_{j_{m+1}+1}
.∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

29
.

Proof of
Lemma

29
.
Let
V=\{v_{0},\dots,v_{m+1}\}
such that, there exist
j_{0},j_{1},\dots,j_{m+1}
with
L_{j_{0}}(v_{0})=\ell_{f}
,
L_{j_{m}}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
and for all
1\leq k\leq m
, there exists
i\in\{0,1,2\}
with
L_{j_{k}}(v_{k})=\textsf{z}^{i}
.

From
Lemma

33
there exists
j^{\prime}_{0}<\dots<j^{\prime}_{m+1}
with
C_{j^{\prime}_{k}}\xrightarrow{b_{k}}C_{j^{\prime}_{k+1}}
for all
0\leq k\leq m+1
, and
(m+1)\mod 3=2
.
Furthermore,
L_{j^{\prime}_{1}}(v_{0})=q_{1}
and
L_{j^{\prime}_{1}+1}(v_{0})=q_{2}
. In the same way, for all
1\leq k\leq m
,
L_{j^{\prime}_{k+1}}(v_{k})=q_{2}^{b_{k}}
and
L_{j^{\prime}_{k+1}+1}(v_{k})=q_{3}^{b_{k}}
. Lastly,
L_{j^{\prime}_{m+1}+1}(v_{m+1})=q_{2}^{\textsf{tail}}
. Hence, by construction of
P
, there exist
j^{\prime\prime}_{0}\dots j^{\prime\prime}_{m},j^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}
such that
L_{j^{\prime\prime}_{i}}(v_{i})=\textsf{z}^{b_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
, and
L_{j^{\prime\prime}_{0}}(v_{0})=\ell_{in}
and
L_{j^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}}(v_{m+1})=q^{\textsf{tail}}
.

\square

C.3.2
Second step: the simulation.

We are now ready to extract the execution of the machine from the execution between
C
and
C^{\prime}
.
Fix
V=\{v_{0},\dots,v_{m+1}\}
the set of vertices from
Lemma

29
. We forget about nodes not in
V
, as they are useless for
v_{f}
to cover
q_{f}
: either they are
neighbors of some node in
V
and so they are inactive, either they are not connected to
v_{f}
through an inactive neighbor, or they are not connected to
v_{f}
.

Let
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
and
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, we denote
C\xrightarrow{\mid v}^{\ast}C^{\prime}
whenever
C=C^{\prime}
or there exist
(u_{1},t_{1}),\dots(u_{N},t_{N})
, with
N\in{\rm Nature}
and for all
1\leq i\leq N
,
u_{i}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus\{v\}
,
t_{i}\in\Delta
, and
C_{1}\xrightarrow{u_{1},t_{1}}\xrightarrow{u_{2},t_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{u_{N},t_{N}}C^{\prime}
.

Let
\textsf{op}\in\text{OP}
, we denote the set
\text{OK}(\textsf{op})
as follows:

•

\text{OK}(\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1})=\{\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}},\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}\}
for some
\textsf{x}\in X
;

•

\text{OK}(\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1})=\{\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}},\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}}\}
for some
\textsf{x}\in X
;

•

\text{OK}({\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}})=\{\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}\}
for some
\textsf{x}\in X
;

•

\text{OK}({\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}})=\{\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}}\}
for some
\textsf{x}\in X
;

•

\text{OK}({\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}})=\{\overline{\text{test}_{{\textsf{x}}}}\}
for some
\textsf{x}\in X
.

We denote
\text{Act}=\{\textsf{x}+1,\textsf{x}-1,\textsf{x}=0\mid\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}\}
.

We start by some observations.

Lemma 34
.

For all
0<i<m+1
, the word broadcast by
v_{i}
belongs to the language
E_{i}=b_{i}\cdot\$\cdot(\sum_{\textsf{op}\in\text{OP},\textsf{ok}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op})}\textsf{op}^{b_{i}}\cdot\textsf{ok}^{b_{i}})^{\ast}\cdot\text{done}
. Furthermore, the word broadcast by
v_{0}
belongs to the language
E_{0}=0\cdot\$\cdot(\sum_{\textsf{op}\in\{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1},\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}\mid\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}\}}(\textsf{op}^{0}\cdot\overline{\textsf{op}}^{0}))^{\ast}\cdot\text{done}
.

Proof.

Follows from the construction of
V
and
P
.
∎

We denote
n_{i}
the index from which
v_{i}
broadcasts "done" for all
0\leq i\leq m
. It holds that
n_{0}<\cdots<n_{m}
by construction of
V
.

Lemma 35
.

For all
0\leq i<m
,
0<j_{1}<j_{2}\leq n_{i}
,
\textsf{op}\in\text{OP}
,
\textsf{ok}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op})
, such that
C_{j_{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\textsf{op}^{b_{i}}}C_{j_{1}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{j_{2}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\textsf{ok}^{b_{i}}}C_{j_{2}+1}
there exists a unique
j_{1}<j_{3}<j_{2}
such that
C_{j_{3}}\xrightarrow{v_{i+1},w}C_{j_{3}+1}
for some
w\in\Sigma
. Furthermore, if
\textsf{ok}=\overline{\textsf{op}_{2}}
, then
w=\textsf{op}_{2}^{b_{i+1}}
.

Proof.

We denote
b:=b_{i+1}
for readability’s sake, and denote
\textsf{ok}=\overline{\textsf{op}_{2}}
.
Recall that
n_{i}<n_{i+1}
and
b=b_{i}\oplus 1
. Hence:

•

if
\textsf{op}_{2}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then
L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1})\in\{r^{b}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b,\textsf{x}_{1}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b,\textsf{x}_{2}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},q^{b}_{\textsf{x}}\}
;

•

if
\textsf{op}_{2}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then
L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1})\in\{r^{b}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b,\textsf{x}_{1}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b,\textsf{x}_{2}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},q^{\prime b}_{\textsf{x}}\}
;

•

otherwise,
L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1})\in\{r^{b}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b,\textsf{x}_{1}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b,\textsf{x}_{2}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}}\}
.

Indeed, otherwise,
L_{j_{2}+1}(v_{i+1})=\frownie
as
(L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1}),?\overline{\textsf{op}_{2}}^{b_{i}},\frownie)\in\Delta_{b}
.
Furthermore:

•

if
\textsf{op}=\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then
L_{j_{1}}(v_{i+1})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b},\textsf{y}^{b}\mid\textsf{y}\in\textsf{X}\setminus\{\textsf{x}\}\}
;

•

otherwise,
L_{j_{1}}(v_{i+1})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b},\textsf{y}^{b}\mid\textsf{y}\in\textsf{X}\}
.

Indeed, otherwise,
L_{j_{2}+1}(v_{i+1})=\frownie
as
(L_{j_{1}}(v_{i+1}),?\textsf{op}^{b_{i}},\frownie)\in\Delta_{b}
.
Note that any path between
L_{j_{1}+1}(v_{i+1})
and
L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1})
requires at least one broadcast from
v_{i+1}
, and in particular a broadcast of
\textsf{op}_{2}^{b}
.
Hence, there exists
j_{1}<j_{3}<j_{2}
such that
C_{j_{3}}\xrightarrow{v_{i+1},\textsf{op}_{2}^{b}}C_{j_{3}+1}
, consider the smallest such index. We now show that this is the unique index between
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
from which
v_{i+1}
broadcasts something.

If there exists
j_{1}<j_{4}<j_{3}
such that
C_{j_{4}}\xrightarrow{v_{i+1},w}C_{j_{4}+1}
for some
w\in\Sigma
, and take
j_{4}
the largest such index. By construction of
j_{4}
and
j_{3}
,
L_{j_{4}+1}(v_{i+1})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b},\textsf{y}^{b}\mid\textsf{y}\in\textsf{X}\}
, and so
w\in\text{OK}^{b}
. Hence, it holds that
L_{j_{4}}(v_{i})\nin\{r^{b_{i}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b_{i},\textsf{x}_{1}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},r^{b_{i},\textsf{x}_{2}}_{\textsf{op}_{2}},q^{b_{i}}_{\textsf{x}},q^{\prime b_{i}}_{\textsf{x}}\}
, as otherwise,
(L_{j_{4}}(v_{i}),?w,\frownie)
. This contradicts the fact that
C_{j_{1}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{j_{4}}
and
C_{j_{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\textsf{op}^{b_{i}}}C_{j_{1}+1}
.
With a similar argument, there is no
j_{3}<j_{5}\leq j_{2}
such that
C_{j_{5}}\xrightarrow{v_{i+1},w}C_{j_{5}+1}
for some
w\in\Sigma
.

∎

Lemma 36
.

For all
0\leq i<m
,
0<j_{1}<j_{2}\leq n_{i}
,
\textsf{op}\in\text{OP}
,
w\in\Sigma
, such that
C_{j_{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\overline{\textsf{op}}^{b_{i}}}C_{j_{1}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{j_{2}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},w}C_{j_{2}+1}
, there exists a unique
j_{1}<j_{3}<j_{2}
such that
C_{j_{3}}\xrightarrow{v_{i+1},w^{\prime}}C_{j_{3}+1}
for some
w^{\prime}\in\Sigma
. Furthermore,
w^{\prime}=\textsf{ok}^{{}^{\prime}b}
with
\textsf{ok}^{\prime}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op})
.

Proof.

We denote
b:=b_{i+1}
for readability’s sake.
Note that from
Lemma

34
,
w\in\text{OP}^{b_{i}}\cup\{\text{done}\}
.
Recall that
n_{i}<n_{i+1}
and
b=b_{i}\oplus 1
. Hence,
L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b}\}
, as otherwise
L_{j_{2}+1}(v_{i+1})=\frownie
, hence
(L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1}),?w,\frownie)\in\Delta_{b}
.
Furthermore,

•

if
\textsf{op}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then
L_{j_{1}}(v_{i+1})\in\{r_{\textsf{op}}^{b},r_{\textsf{op}}^{b,\textsf{x}_{1}},r_{\textsf{op}}^{b,\textsf{x}_{2}},q_{\textsf{x}}^{b}\}
;

•

if
\textsf{op}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then
L_{j_{1}}(v_{i+1})\in\{r_{\textsf{op}}^{b},r_{\textsf{op}}^{b,\textsf{x}_{1}},r_{\textsf{op}}^{b,\textsf{x}_{2}},q^{\prime b}_{\textsf{x}}\}
;

•

otherwise,
L_{j_{1}}(v_{i+1})\in\{r_{\textsf{op}}^{b},r_{\textsf{op}}^{b,\textsf{x}_{1}},r_{\textsf{op}}^{b,\textsf{x}_{2}}\}
.

Note that any path between
L_{j_{1}}(v_{i+1})
and
L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1})
requires at least one broadcast from
v_{i+1}
of a message
w^{\prime}
, and if
w^{\prime}
is the last broadcast from
v_{i+1}
before reaching
L_{j_{2}}(v_{i+1})
, it holds that:

•

if
\textsf{op}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
, then
w^{\prime}\in\{\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}^{b},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}^{b}\}
;

•

if
\textsf{op}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}
, then
w^{\prime}\in\{\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}}^{b},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}^{b}\}
;

•

otherwise,
w^{\prime}=\overline{\textsf{op}}^{b}
.

Hence
w^{\prime}=\textsf{ok}^{\prime b}
with
\textsf{ok}^{\prime}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op})
.
We now show that
j_{3}
is the unique index between
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
from which
v_{i+1}
broadcasts something. Remember that it has been chosen as the greatest such index.

If there exists
j_{1}<j_{4}<j_{3}
such that
C_{j_{4}}\xrightarrow{v_{i+1},w^{\prime\prime}}C_{j_{4}+1}
for some
w^{\prime\prime}\in\Sigma
, and take
j_{4}
the largest such index. By construction of
j_{3}
and
j_{4}
,
L_{j_{4}+1}(v_{i+1})\in\{r_{\textsf{op}}^{b},r_{\textsf{op}}^{b,\textsf{x}},q_{\textsf{x}}^{b},q^{\prime b}_{\textsf{x}}\mid\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}\}
, and so
w^{\prime\prime}\in\text{OP}^{b}
. Hence, it holds that
L_{j_{4}}(v_{i})\nin\{r^{b_{i}}_{\textsf{op}},r^{b_{i},\textsf{x}}_{\textsf{op}}\mid\textsf{op}\in\text{OP},\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}\}
, as otherwise,
(L_{j_{4}}(v_{i}),?w,\frownie)
. This contradicts the fact that
C_{j_{1}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{j_{4}}
and
C_{j_{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\overline{\textsf{op}}^{b_{i}}}C_{j_{1}+1}
.

∎

Lemma 37
.

Let
0<j_{1}^{0}<j_{2}^{0}<n_{0}
and
\textsf{op}\in\{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1},\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}\mid\textsf{x}\in X\}
such that
C_{j_{1}^{0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},w_{0}}C_{j_{1}^{0}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j_{2}^{0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},w^{\prime}_{0}}C_{j_{2}^{0}+1}
and
w_{0}=\textsf{op}^{0}
and
w^{\prime}_{0}=\overline{\textsf{op}}^{0}
. Then there exists a unique
sequence of indices

j_{1}^{1},j_{2}^{1},\dots,j_{1}^{m},j_{2}^{m}
such that for all
1\leq i\leq m
:

•

j_{1}^{i-1}<j_{1}^{i}<j_{2}^{i-1}<j_{2}^{i}<n_{i}
, and

•

C_{j_{1}^{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},w_{i}}C_{j_{1}^{i}+1}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{j_{2}^{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}}C_{j_{2}^{i}+1}
for some
w_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}\in\Sigma
, and

•

if
w_{i-1}=\overline{\textsf{op}_{i}}^{b_{i-1}}
for some
\textsf{op}_{i}\in\text{OP}
, then
w_{i}=\textsf{op}_{i}^{b_{i}}
and
w^{\prime}_{i}=\textsf{ok}_{i}^{b_{i}}
with
\textsf{ok}_{i}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op}_{i})
.

Proof.

Let
0<j_{1}^{0}<j_{2}^{0}<n_{0}
and
\textsf{op}\in\{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1},\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}\mid\textsf{x}\in X\}
such that
C_{j_{1}^{0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},\textsf{op}^{0}}C_{j_{1}^{0}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j_{2}^{0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},\overline{\textsf{op}}^{0}}C_{j_{2}^{0}+1}
.

We build the sequence of indexes
j_{1}^{1},j_{2}^{1},\dots,j_{1}^{m},j_{2}^{m}
and the sequence of operations
\textsf{op}_{1},\dots,\textsf{op}_{m}
such that
w_{i}=\textsf{op}_{i}^{b_{i}}
and
w^{\prime}_{i}=\textsf{ok}_{i}^{b_{i}}
with
\textsf{ok}_{i}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op}_{i})
inductively.
Using
Lemma

35
, there exists a unique
j_{1}^{0}<j_{1}^{1}<j_{2}^{0}
such that
C_{j_{1}^{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{1},w_{1}}C_{j_{1}^{1}+1}
for some
w_{1}\in\Sigma
, and it holds that
w_{1}=\textsf{op}^{1}
. As
j_{2}^{0}<n_{0}
, there exists
j_{2}^{0}<n\leq n_{0}
such that
C_{n}\xrightarrow{v_{0},u}C_{n+1}
for some
u\in\Sigma
. Using
Lemma

36
, there exists a unique
j_{2}^{0}<j_{2}^{1}<n
such that
C_{j_{2}^{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}}C_{j_{2}^{1}+1}
for some
w^{\prime}_{1}\in\Sigma
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=\textsf{ok}_{1}^{1}
and
\textsf{ok}_{1}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op})
.

Define
\textsf{op}_{1}=\textsf{op}
.
We found a unique pair of indexes
j_{1}^{1},j_{2}^{1}
such that
j_{1}^{0}<j_{1}^{1}<j_{2}^{0}<j_{2}^{1}\leq n_{0}<n_{1}
and
C_{j_{1}^{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{1},w_{1}}C_{j_{1}^{1}+1}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{j_{2}^{1}}\xrightarrow{v_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}}C_{j_{2}^{1}+1}
for some
w_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}\in\Sigma
. Furthermore,
w^{\prime}_{0}=\overline{\textsf{op}}^{0}
and
w_{1}=\textsf{op}_{1}^{1}
and
w^{\prime}_{1}=\textsf{ok}_{1}^{b}
with
\textsf{ok}_{1}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op}_{1})
.

Assume we have built
j_{1}^{1},j_{2}^{1},\dots,j_{1}^{k},j_{2}^{k}
for some
k<m
.
And denote
C_{j_{1}^{k}}\xrightarrow{v_{k},\textsf{op}_{k}^{b_{k}}}C_{j_{1}^{k}+1}
and
C_{j_{2}^{k}}\xrightarrow{v_{k},\textsf{ok}_{k}^{b_{k}}}C_{j_{2}^{k}+1}
with
\textsf{ok}_{k}=\overline{\textsf{op}^{\prime}_{k}}
.
Using
Lemma

35
, there exists a unique
j_{1}^{k}<j_{1}^{k+1}<j_{2}^{k}
such that
C_{j_{1}^{k+1}}\xrightarrow{v_{k+1},w_{k+1}}C_{j_{1}^{1}+1}
for some
w_{k+1}\in\Sigma
. Furthermore
w_{k+1}=\textsf{op}_{k}^{{}^{\prime}b_{k+1}}
.

As
j_{2}^{k}<n_{k}
, there exists
j_{2}^{k}<n\leq n_{k}
such that
C_{n}\xrightarrow{v_{k},u}C_{n+1}
for some
u\in\Sigma
. Using
Lemma

36
, there exists a unique
j_{2}^{k}<j_{2}^{k+1}<n\leq n_{k}<n_{k+1}
such that
C_{j_{2}^{k+1}}\xrightarrow{v_{k+1},w^{\prime}_{k+1}}C_{j_{2}^{k+1}+1}
for some
w^{\prime}_{k+1}\in\Sigma
. Furthermore,
w^{\prime}_{k+1}=\textsf{ok}^{{}^{\prime}b}
and
\textsf{ok}^{\prime}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op}^{\prime}_{k})
.

∎

Let
0<j_{1}^{0}<j_{2}^{0}<n_{0}
and
\textsf{op}\in\{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1},\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}\mid\textsf{x}\in X\}
such that
C_{j_{1}^{0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},\textsf{op}^{0}}C_{j_{1}^{0}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j_{2}^{0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},\overline{\textsf{op}}^{0}}C_{j_{2}^{0}+1}
.
We denote
\textsf{Seq}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})
the unique sequence of indexes
j_{1}^{1},j_{2}^{1},\dots,\linebreak j_{1}^{m},j_{2}^{m}
defined in the previous lemma.

Lemma 38
.

If
\textsf{op}=\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
L_{j_{1}^{i}}(v_{i})=L_{j_{2}^{i}+1}(v_{i})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{i}},\textsf{y}^{b_{i}}\mid\textsf{y}\neq\textsf{x}\}
.

Proof.

From
Lemma

37
, as
\text{OK}(\text{test}_{\textsf{x}})=\{\overline{\text{test}_{{\textsf{x}}}}\}
, it holds that
for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
C_{j_{1}^{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}^{b_{i}}}C_{j_{1}^{i}+1}
, and
C_{j_{2}^{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\overline{\text{test}_{{\textsf{x}}}}^{b_{i}}}C_{j_{2}^{i}+1}
. As
C_{j_{1}^{i}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{j_{2}^{i}}
, by construction of the protocol, we have that that
L_{j_{1}^{i}}(v_{i})=L_{j_{2}^{i}+1}(v_{i})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{i}},\textsf{y}^{b_{i}}\mid\textsf{y}\neq\textsf{x}\}
.
∎

Lemma 39
.

If
\textsf{op}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then, there exists a unique
1\leq p\leq m
such that
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})=\textsf{z}^{b_{p}}
,
L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})=\textsf{x}^{b_{p}}
, and for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
i\neq p
, it holds that

L_{j_{1}^{i}}(v_{i})=L_{j_{2}^{i}+1}(v_{i})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{i}},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{i}},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{i}}\}
.

Proof.

Denote
\textsf{op}_{0},\dots,\textsf{op}_{m}\in\text{OP}
,
\textsf{ok}_{0},\dots,\textsf{ok}_{m}\in\text{OK}
as defined in
Lemma

37
.

We start by oberving the following:
Let
0\leq i<m
such that
\textsf{op}_{i}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
, then
\textsf{op}_{i+1}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
and
\textsf{ok}_{i+1}=\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
. Indeed, it holds that
\textsf{ok}_{i}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
by definition of
\text{OK}({\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}})
, hence .
by
Lemma

37
,
\textsf{op}_{i+1}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
, and so
\textsf{ok}_{i+1}=\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
by definition of
\text{OK}({\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}})
.

As a consequence,
L_{j_{1}^{i+1}}(v_{i+1})=L_{j_{2}^{i+1}+1}(v_{i+1})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{i+1}},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{i+1}},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{i+1}}\}
.

Now let
0<i\leq m
, such that
\textsf{op}_{i}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
, then
\textsf{op}_{i-1}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
and
\textsf{ok}_{i-1}=\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
.
Indeed, by
Lemma

37
, as
\textsf{op}_{i}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
, it must be that
\textsf{ok}_{i-1}=\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
. And from
34
,
\textsf{ok}_{i-1}\in\text{OK}(\textsf{op}_{i-1})
.
Note that the only
\textsf{op}\in\text{OP}
such that
\overline{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}\in\text{OP}(\textsf{op})
is
\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
, hence
\textsf{op}_{i-1}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
.

As a consequence,
L_{j_{1}^{i-1}}(v_{i+1})=L_{j_{2}^{i-1}+1}(v_{i-1})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{i-1}},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{i-1}},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{i-1}}\}
.

Recall that
\textsf{op}_{0}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
and observe that
\textsf{op}_{m}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
, as otherwise, necessarily
L_{j_{1}^{m}+1}(v_{m+1})=\frownie
. As a consequence, there exists a unique
0<p<m
such that
\textsf{op}_{p}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
and
\textsf{op}_{p+1}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
and it holds that for all
i<p
,
\textsf{op}_{i}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
and for all
i>p
,
\textsf{op}_{i}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
.

Observe that, from
Lemma

37
, as
\textsf{op}_{p+1}={\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
,
\textsf{ok}_{p}=\overline{\text{done}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}}
, and so by construction of
P
, it holds that
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})=\textsf{z}^{b_{p}}
,
L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})=\textsf{x}^{b_{p}}
.

∎

Lemma 40
.

If
\textsf{op}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}
for some
\textsf{x}\in\mathsf{X}
, then, there exists a unique
1\leq p\leq m
such that
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})=\textsf{x}^{b_{p}}
,
L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})=\textsf{z}^{b_{p}}
, and for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
i\neq p
, it holds that

L_{j_{1}^{i}}(v_{i})=L_{j_{2}^{i}+1}(v_{i})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{i}},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{i}},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{i}}\}
.

Proof.

Same proof as
Lemma

39
.
∎

We now associate to
j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0}
two configurations
\textsf{prec}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})
,
\textsf{succ}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})
of the machine as follows:

•

\ell=L_{j_{1}^{0}}(v_{0})
,
x_{1}=|\{i\mid L_{j_{1}^{i}}(v_{i})=\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{i}},1\leq i\leq m\}|
,
x_{2}=|\{i\mid L_{j_{1}^{i}}(v_{i})=\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{i}},1\leq i\leq m\}|
;

•

\ell^{\prime}=L_{j_{2}^{0}+1}(v_{0})
,
x^{\prime}_{1}=|\{i\mid L_{j_{2}^{i}+1}(v_{i})=\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{i}},1\leq i\leq m\}|
,
x^{\prime}_{2}=|\{i\mid L_{j_{2}^{i}+1}(v_{i})=\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{i}},1\leq i\leq m\}|
.

Lemma 41
.

\textsf{prec}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})\xrightarrow{}\textsf{succ}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})

Proof.

Denote
\textsf{op}_{0},\dots,\textsf{op}_{m}\in\text{OP}
,
\textsf{ok}_{0},\dots,\textsf{ok}_{m}\in\text{OK}
as defined in
Lemma

37
.

Observe that by construction,
L_{j_{1}^{0}}(v_{0})=\ell
,
L_{j_{2}^{0}+1}(v_{0})=\ell^{\prime}
for some
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\text{Loc}
.

•

If
\textsf{op}_{0}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1}
, then by construction, there exists
t=(\ell,\text{inc(}\textsf{x}\text{)},\ell^{\prime})\in\text{Trans}
. Furthermore, by
Lemma

39
, there exists a unique
1\leq p<m
such that
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})\neq L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})
and
L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})=\textsf{x}^{b_{p}}
and
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})=\textsf{z}^{b_{p}}
. Denote
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\textsf{x}=\textsf{x}_{i}
and
\textsf{prec}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})
.
Hence,
\textsf{succ}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})
with
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}+1
, and
x^{\prime}_{3-i}=x_{3-i}
.

•

If
\textsf{op}_{0}=\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}
, then by construction, there exists
t=(\ell,\text{test(}\textsf{x}\text{)},\ell^{\prime})\in\text{Trans}
. Denote
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\textsf{x}=\textsf{x}_{i}

By
Lemma

38
, for all
1\leq k\leq m
,
L_{j_{1}^{k}}(v_{k})=L_{j_{2}^{k}+1}(v_{k})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{k}},\textsf{x}_{3-i}^{b_{k}}\}
. Hence, if
\textsf{prec}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})
, then
x_{i}=0
. Denote
\textsf{succ}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})
, it holds that
x^{\prime}_{1}=x_{1}
,
x_{2}=x^{\prime}_{2}
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}=0
.

•

If
\textsf{op}_{0}=\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1}
, then by construction, there exists
t=(\ell,\text{dec(}\textsf{x}\text{)},\ell^{\prime})\in\text{Trans}
. Furthermore, by
Lemma

40
, there exists a unique
1\leq p<m
such that
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})\neq L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})
and furthermore,
L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})=\textsf{z}^{b_{p}}
and
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})=\textsf{x}^{b_{p}}
. Denote
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\textsf{x}=\textsf{x}_{i}
and
\textsf{prec}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})
.
Hence,
\textsf{succ}(j_{1}^{0},j_{2}^{0})=(\ell^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})
with
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}-1
, and
x^{\prime}_{3-i}=x_{3-i}
.

∎

We now denote
j<j_{1,0}<j_{2,0}<\cdots<j_{1,k}<j_{2,k}<n_{0}
for some
k\in{\rm Nature}
the indices such that:

C_{0}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j}\xrightarrow{v_{0},0}C_{j+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j_{1,0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},{\textsf{op}^{0}}^{0}}C_{j_{1,0}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j_{2,0}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},\overline{\textsf{op}^{0}}^{0}}C_{j_{2,0}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j_{1,1}}\cdots C_{j_{2,k}}\xrightarrow{v_{0},\overline{\textsf{op}^{k}}^{0}}C_{j_{2,k}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{n_{0}}
with
\textsf{op}^{0},\dots,\textsf{op}^{k}\in\{\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}+1},\text{todo}_{{\textsf{x}}-1},\text{test}_{\textsf{x}}\mid\textsf{x}\in\{\textsf{x}_{1},\textsf{x}_{2}\}\}
.

Lemma 42
.

\textsf{prec}(j_{1,0},j_{2,0})=(\ell_{in},0,0)
.

Proof.

Denote
\textsf{Seq}(j_{1,0},j_{2,0})=j_{1}^{1},j_{2}^{1},\dots,j_{1}^{m},j_{2}^{m}
.

First note that by construction,
L_{j_{1}^{0}}(v_{0})=\ell_{in}
.

In fact: for all
1\leq i\leq m
, it holds that
C_{0}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{x_{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},b_{i}}C_{x_{i}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{y_{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\$}C_{y_{i}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{j_{1}^{i}}
for some indices
x_{i},y_{i}
. Assume this is not the case and take the smallest index
p
such that this is not the case: there exists at least two indices from which it broadcasts
b_{p}
and
\$
as
L_{j_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})\in\{\textsf{z}^{p},\textsf{x}_{1}^{p},\textsf{x}_{2}^{p}\}
. Assume now, there are more than two indices from which
v_{p}
broadcasts, i.e. there exist
x_{p}<y_{p}<z_{p}
such that

C_{0}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{p}}^{\ast}C_{x_{p}}\xrightarrow{v_{p},b_{p}}C_{x_{p}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{p}}^{\ast}C_{y_{p}}\xrightarrow{v_{p},\$}C_{y_{p}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{p}}^{\ast}C_{z_{p}}\xrightarrow{v_{p},w}C_{z_{p}+1}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{j_{1}^{p}}
. By construction of the protocol,
w\in\text{OP}^{b_{p}}
.
If
p=1
, then
L_{z_{p}}(v_{0})=\ell_{in}
, and so
L_{z_{p}+1}(v_{0})=\frownie
which contradicts the existence of
j_{1,0}
.
Assume
p>1
, then
L_{z_{p}}(v_{p-1})=\textsf{z}^{b_{p-1}}
and so
L_{z_{p}+1}(v_{p-1})=\frownie
which contradicts the fact that
v^{p-1}
broadcasts "done".

Hence, for all
1\leq i\leq m
, it holds that
C_{0}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{x_{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},b_{i}}C_{x_{i}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{y_{i}}\xrightarrow{v_{i},\$}C_{y_{i}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{i}}^{\ast}C_{j_{1}^{i}}
for some indices
x_{i},y_{i}
. As a consequence,
L_{j_{1}^{i}}(v_{i})=\textsf{z}^{b_{i}}
, and so
\textsf{prec}(j_{1,0},j_{2,0})=(\ell_{in},0,0)
.
∎

Lemma 43
.

For all
0\leq i<k
,
\textsf{succ}(j_{1,i},j_{2,i})=\textsf{prec}(j_{1,i+1},j_{2,i+1})
.

Proof.

Let
0\leq i<k
such that
\textsf{succ}(j_{1,i},j_{2,i})\neq\textsf{prec}(j_{1,i+1},j_{2,i+1})
.
Denote
\textsf{succ}(j_{1,i},j_{2,i})=(\ell,x_{1},x_{2})
and
\textsf{prec}(j_{1,i+1},j_{2,i+1})=(\ell^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})
.
Denote
\textsf{Seq}(j_{1,i},j_{2,i})=j_{1}^{1},j_{2}^{1},\dots,j_{1}^{m},j_{2}^{m}
and
\textsf{Seq}(j_{1,i+1},j_{2,i+1})=i_{1}^{1},i_{2}^{1},\dots,i_{1}^{m},i_{2}^{m}
.
By construction,
C_{j_{2,i}+1}\xrightarrow{\mid v_{0}}^{\ast}C_{j_{1,i+1}}
hence,
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
.
Hence, there exists
1\leq p\leq m
such that
L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})\neq L_{i_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})
. Consider the first such index
p
.
By
37
,
L_{j_{2}^{p}+1}(v_{p})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{p}},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{p}},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{p}}\}
. Again, by
37
,
L_{i_{1}^{p}}(v_{p})\neq\frownie
and so there exists
j_{2}^{p}+1\leq j<i_{1}^{p}
such that
C_{j}\xrightarrow{v_{p},w}C_{j+1}
. Take
j
the first such index, by construction of
P
,
w\in\text{OP}^{b_{p}}
.
If
p=1
, then
L_{j+1}(v_{0})=\frownie
, as
w\in\text{OP}^{b_{p}}
and
L_{j+1}(v_{0})=L_{j_{1,i}}(v_{0})=\ell
.
If
p>1
, then
L_{j}(v_{p-1})=L_{j_{2}^{p-1}+1}(v_{p-1})\in\{\textsf{z}^{b_{p-1}},\textsf{x}_{1}^{b_{p-1}},\textsf{x}_{2}^{b_{p-1}}\}
. Hence,
L_{j+1}(v_{p-1})=\frownie
which contradicts the existence of
i_{1}^{p-1}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

28
:

Proof of
Lemma

28
.

We found a sequence of indices
j<j_{1,0}<j_{2,0}<\cdots<j_{1,k}<j_{2,k}<n_{0}
such that:

•

\textsf{prec}(j_{1,0},j_{2,0})=(\ell_{in},0,0)
(
Lemma

42
)

•

for all
0\leq j\leq k
,
\textsf{prec}(j_{1,j},j_{2,j})\xrightarrow{}\textsf{succ}(j_{1,j},j_{2,j})
(
Lemma

41
)

•

for all
0\leq i<k
,
\textsf{succ}(j_{1,i},j_{2,i})=\textsf{prec}(j_{1,i+1},j_{2,i+1})
(
Lemma

43
)

•

\textsf{succ}(j_{1,k},j_{2,k})=(\ell_{f},x_{1},x_{2})
for some
x_{1},x_{2}\in{\rm Nature}
, as
L_{j_{2,k}}(v_{0})=L_{n_{0}}(v_{0})=\ell_{f}
.

Putting everything together, we get a sequence
(\ell_{in},0,0)\xrightarrow{}\textsf{succ}(j_{1,0},j_{2,0})\xrightarrow{}\textsf{succ}(j_{1,1},j_{2,1})\xrightarrow{}\cdots\xrightarrow{}\textsf{succ}(j_{1,k},j_{2,k})=(\ell_{f},x_{1},x_{2})
.

\square

.

in

. Both are b-configurations as the root node

. The broadcast-print of

D.1
Proof of
Lemma

11

Proof of
Lemma

11
.

Assume there exists
\Gamma=(V,E)
in
Graphs
,
C=(\Gamma,L)\in\mathcal{I}
and
D=(\Gamma,L^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
and
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}D
and
L^{\prime}(v)=q_{f}
. Thanks to
Theorem

4
, we can assume that
\Gamma\in\textsf{Trees}
and without loss of generality that
v=\epsilon
(i.e. it the root of the tree). We consider the execution:

C_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{1},\delta_{1}}C_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{2},\delta_{2}}C_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{3},\delta_{3}}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{n},\delta_{n}}C_{n}

such that
C_{0}=C
and
C_{n}=D
and
C_{i}=(\Gamma,L_{i})
for all
i\in[0,n]
. Note in particular that
L_{n}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
. Without loss of generality we can as assume that
|v_{n}|\leq 1
otherwise it means that the last step of this execution does not affect the root node and hence we could have stop it at
C_{n-1}
. We denote by
m
the number of steps in this executions labelled by the root node or the node at height
1
whose label in the previous step belongs to
Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
, i.e.
m=|\{i\in[1,n]\mid v_{i}=\epsilon\mbox{ or }(|v_{i}|=1\mbox{ and }L_{i-1}(v_{i})\in Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1})\}|
. Intuitively
m
is the number of steps in the execution, which can affect the root node or which an internal move of a node at height
1
which stays in the
Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
component. Note that since we are dealing with 1-phase-bounded protocol if for some
i\in[1,n]
and some nodes
v\in V
, we have
L_{i}(v)\notin Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
then
L_{j}(v)\notin Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
for all
j\in[i,n]
.
We consider then the configuration
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\textsf{Stars}
which is a restriction of the tree
\Gamma
to nodes of height at most one, i.e.
\Gamma^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
with
V^{\prime}=\{v\in V\mid|v|\leq 1\}
and
E^{\prime}=\{(v,v^{\prime})\in E\mid v,v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}\}
.

We will show how to build an execution of length
m
from
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}
to a configuration
D^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime\prime})
with
L^{\prime\prime\prime}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
. For this matter we consider an injective function
f:[1,m]\mapsto[1,n]
which select
m
indices of the step of the previously introduced execution keeping only the one labelled by nodes in
V^{\prime}
. Formally
f
is the unique injective function respecting the following conditions:

•

for all
i\in[1,m]
,
v_{f(i)}=\epsilon
or
(|v_{f(i)}|=1
and
L_{f(i)-1}(v_{f(i)})\in Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
) ;

•

for all
i,j\in[1,m]
, if
i<j
then
f(i)<f(j)
.

Since
v_{n}\in V^{\prime}
, we have
f(m)=n
. We shall now show that there exists an execution of the form:

C^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{f(1)},\delta_{f(1)}}C^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{f(2)},\delta_{f(2)}}C^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{f(3)},\delta_{f(3)}}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{f(m)},\delta_{f(m)}}C^{\prime}_{m}

where
C^{\prime}_{0}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{0})
and for all
i\in[1,m]
we have
C^{\prime}_{i}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{i})
with
L^{\prime}_{i}
satisfying the following condition:
L_{i}^{\prime}(\epsilon)=L_{f(i)}(\epsilon)
and for all
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{\epsilon\}
, if
L_{f(i)}(v)\in Q^{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
then
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=L_{f(i)}(v)
. We shall now see how to build this execution by induction. First note that if we extend
f
to
0
by setting
f(0)=0
for
i=0
, we have that
L_{0}^{\prime}(\epsilon)=L_{f(0)}(\epsilon)=q_{in}
and for all
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{\epsilon\}
,
L^{\prime}_{0}(v)=L_{f(0)}(v)=q_{in}
and
q_{in}\in Q^{0}
. Now assume that we have build

C^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{f(1)},\delta_{f(1)}}C^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{f(2)},\delta_{f(2)}}C^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{f(3)},\delta_{f(3)}}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{f(i)},\delta_{f(i)}}C^{\prime}_{i}
with
C^{\prime}_{i}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{i})
with
L^{\prime}_{i}
satisfying the following condition:
L_{i}^{\prime}(\epsilon)=L_{f(i)}(\epsilon)
and for all
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{\epsilon\}
, if
L_{f(i)}(v)\in Q^{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
then
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=L_{f(i)}(v)
. Consider the node
v_{f(i+1)}
, we have two cases:

1.

v_{f(i+1)}=\epsilon
. Since between
C_{f(i)}
and
C_{f(i+1)-1}
the root node did not perform an internal action, nor any broadcast and none of the node at height 1 performed a broadcast, we deduce that
L_{f(i+1)-1}(v_{f(i+1)})=L_{f(i)}(v_{f(i+1)})=L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{f(i+1)})
, hence from
C^{\prime}_{i}
the transition
\delta_{f(i+1)}
can be taken from the root node. And we have
C^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{v_{f(i+1)},\delta_{f(i+1)}}C^{\prime}_{i+1}
with
C^{\prime}_{i+1}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{i+1})
. Note that we have necessarily
L_{i+1}^{\prime}(\epsilon)=L_{f(i+1)}(\epsilon)
. And for all
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{\epsilon\}
, if
L_{f(i+1)}(v)\in Q^{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
, it means that the action of the root node did not affect
v
then
L_{f(i+1)}(v)=L_{f(i+1)-1}(v)
and by the same reasoning as we did for the root node, since
L_{f(i+1)-1}(v)\in Q^{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
, we have
L_{f(i+1)-1}(v)=L_{f(i)}(v)=L^{\prime}_{i}(v)
. But in the transition from
C^{\prime}_{i}
to
C^{\prime}_{i+1}
the same action still not affect
v
, this allows us to deduce that
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L^{\prime}_{i}(v)
. Hence
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L_{f(i+1)}(v)
.

2.

v_{f(i+1)}\neq\epsilon
. In that case
|v_{f(i+1)}|=1
and
L_{f(i+1)-1}(v_{f(i+1)})\in Q_{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
. Here again we can deduce that
L_{f(i+1)-1}(v_{f(i+1)})=L_{f(i)}(v_{f(i+1)})=L^{\prime}_{i}(v_{f(i+1)})
, hence from
C^{\prime}_{i}
the transition
\delta_{f(i+1)}
can be taken from the node
v_{f(i+1)}
. Hence we have
C^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{v_{f(i+1)},\delta_{f(i+1)}}C^{\prime}_{i+1}
with
C^{\prime}_{i+1}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{i+1})
. Consider
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{\epsilon\}
, if
v=v_{f(i+1)}
, it is clear that
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L_{f(i+1)}
. If
v\neq v_{f(i+1)}
and
L_{f(i+1)}(v)\in Q^{0}\cup Q^{b}_{1}
, we can deduce that
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L^{\prime}_{i}(v)
because the action of node at height 1 does not affect the other node at height 1, and
L_{f(i+1)}(v)=L_{f(i+1)-1}(v)
for the same reason, and
L_{f(i+1)-1}(v)=L_{f(i)}(v)
by definition of
f
and
L_{f(i)}(v)=L^{\prime}_{i}(v)
. Hence
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L_{f(i+1)}(v)
. If
v=\epsilon
, we can show that
L^{\prime}_{i}(v)=L_{f(i)}(v)=L_{f(i+1)-1}(v)
and this allows us to deduce that
L^{\prime}_{i+1}(v)=L_{f(i+1)}(v)
.

We have hence proven that we can build the execution
C^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{f(1)},\delta_{f(1)}}C^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{f(2)},\delta_{f(2)}}C^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{f(3)},\delta_{f(3)}}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{f(m)},\delta_{f(m)}}C^{\prime}_{m}
where
C^{\prime}_{m}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{m})
such that
L^{\prime}_{m}(\epsilon)=L_{f(m)}(\epsilon)=L_{n}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
.

\square

D.2
Proof of
Lemma

12

Proof of
Lemma

12
.

Let
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
. We consider
F
subset of
Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
, built according to the following rules:

•

Initially,
F=\emptyset
;

•

for all transitions
(q,\tau,q^{\prime})\in\Delta
, add
(q^{\prime},\Lambda)
to
F
;

•

for all transitions
(q,!!m,q^{\prime})\in\Delta
, add
(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
to
F
where
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda\setminus\{q^{\prime\prime}\in\Lambda\mid\exists q^{\prime\prime\prime}\in Q.(q^{\prime\prime},?m,q^{\prime\prime\prime})\in\Delta\}
;

•

for all transitions
(q^{\prime},\tau,q^{\prime\prime})\in\Delta
, with
q^{\prime}\in\Lambda
add
(q,\Lambda\cup\{q^{\prime\prime}\})
and
(q,(\Lambda\setminus\{q^{\prime}\})\cup\{q^{\prime\prime}\})
to
F
(the latter case being here to deal with the case where in the configuration with broadcast-print
(q,\Lambda)
there is a single node labelled with
q^{\prime}
);

•

for all transitions
(q^{\prime},!!m,q^{\prime\prime})\in\Delta
such that
q^{\prime}\in\Lambda
and there does not exist
q^{\prime\prime\prime}\in Q
with
(q,?m,q^{\prime\prime\prime})\in\Delta
, add
(q,\Lambda\cup\{q^{\prime\prime}\})
and
(q,(\Lambda\setminus\{q^{\prime}\})\cup\{q^{\prime\prime}\})
to
F
.

It is clear that
F
can be built in polynomial time and a case analysis allows to show
F=\{(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\mid(q,\Lambda)\Rightarrow(q^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\}
.

\square

D.3
Proof of
Lemma

13

Proof of
Lemma

13
.

(i)

Let
C_{1}=((V_{1},E_{1}),L_{1})
,
C^{\prime}_{1}=((V_{1},E_{1}),L_{1})
and
C_{2}=((V_{2},E_{2}),L_{2})
be star-configurations such that
C_{1}\xrightarrow{v,\delta}C^{\prime}_{1}
and
C_{1}\preceq C_{2}
. First note that if
v\neq\epsilon
and
L_{1}(v)\notin Q^{b}
, then
C^{\prime}_{1}\preceq C_{2}
and
C_{2}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{2}
. If
v=\epsilon
, then
L_{2}(v)=L_{1}(v)
by definition of
\preceq
, hence we have
C_{2}\xrightarrow{v,\delta}C^{\prime}_{2}
and one can show that
C^{\prime}_{1}\preceq C^{\prime}_{2}
(the root node have obviously the same label and if some nodes at height one are affected because of a broadcast from
C_{1}
to
C^{\prime}_{1}
, their label will go out of
Q^{b}
in
C^{\prime}_{1}
and the nodes with the same label will be affected similarly going to
C^{\prime}_{2}
). If
v\neq\epsilon
and
L_{1}(v)\notin Q^{b}
, then there exists a node
v^{\prime}\in V_{2}\setminus\{\epsilon\}
such that
L_{2}(v^{\prime})=L_{1}(v)
and we have
C_{2}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime},\delta}C^{\prime}_{2}
with
C^{\prime}_{1}\preceq C^{\prime}_{2}
(here if
\delta
is a broadcast it should affect the root node from
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
similarly and does not affect the other nodes).

(ii)

Assume
C_{1}=((V_{1},E_{1}),L_{1})
,
C^{\prime}_{1}=((V_{1},E_{1}),L_{1})
and
C_{2}=((V_{2},E_{2}),L_{2})
are
b-configurations such that
C_{1}\xrightarrow{v,\delta}C^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathbf{bprint}(C_{1})=\mathbf{bprint}(C_{2})
and
C_{1}\preceq C_{2}
. From the previous point, we easily deduce that there
exists a b-configuration
C^{\prime}_{2}
such that
C^{\prime}_{1}\preceq C^{\prime}_{2}
and
C_{2}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{2}
. However it might be the case that
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{1})\neq\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{2})
. In that case, we have necessarily
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{1})=(q,\Lambda_{1})
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{2})=(q,\Lambda_{2})
with
\Lambda_{1}\subset\Lambda_{2}
. This happens if
v\neq\epsilon
is the only node labelled by
L_{1}(v)\in Q^{b}
in
C_{1}
and in
C_{2}
there are strictly more than
1
node labelled by
L_{1}(v)
. Assume
\{v^{\prime}\in V_{2}\mid L_{2}(v)=L_{1}(v)\}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
then the execution
C_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{1},\delta}C_{2,1}\xrightarrow{v_{2},\delta}C_{2,2}\xrightarrow{v_{3},\delta}C_{2,3}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{k},\delta}C_{2,k}
is a valid execution. Note that along this execution the state of the root node does not change, because
C^{\prime}_{1}
is a b-configuration hence
C_{1}\xrightarrow{v,\delta}C^{\prime}_{1}
with
v\neq\epsilon
does not affect the root node (otherwise it would perform a reception and its state would not be in
Q^{b}
anymore). Furthermore we have
C^{\prime}_{1}\preceq C^{\prime}_{2,k}
and
C_{2}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{2,k}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{1})=\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{2,k})
.

(iii)

Let
C=((V,E),L),
be a b-configuration such that
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C
for some initial configuration
C_{in}
and such that
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
. Let
N\in{\rm Nature}
. We assume that
\Lambda=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\}
. There exist
k
nodes
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
in
V\setminus\{\epsilon\}
such that
L(u_{i})=q_{i}
for all
i\in[1,k]
. Assume furthermore that we have an execution of the form:

C_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{1},\delta_{1}}C_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{2},\delta_{2}}C_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{3},\delta_{3}}\cdots\xrightarrow{v_{n},\delta_{n}}C_{n}

with
C_{0}=C_{in}
and
C_{n}=C
. Here again since
C
is a b-configuration and
q_{i}\in Q^{b}
for all
i\in[1,k]
, none of the transitions
\delta_{i}
performed by a node
v_{i}
different than
\epsilon
changes the state of
\epsilon
and none of the transitions performed by
\epsilon
changes the states of nodes
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
. We build an initial star configuration
C^{\prime}_{in}=((V^{\prime},E^{\prime}),L^{\prime}_{in})
where
V^{\prime}=V\cup\{w_{1,1},w_{1,2},\ldots,w_{1,N},w_{2,1},w_{2,2},\ldots,w_{2,N},\ldots,w_{k,1},w_{k,2},\ldots,w_{k,N}\}
with
w_{i,j}
is a node of depth one such that
w_{i,j}=\max(v\in V\setminus\{\epsilon\})+(i-1)*N+j
and
E^{\prime}
is defined such that
(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a star with root
\epsilon
. Now we build an execution from
C^{\prime}_{in}
to
C^{\prime}=((V^{\prime},E^{\prime}),L^{\prime})
following the same steps as the execution from
C_{in}
to
C
and replacing each step
\xrightarrow{v_{\ell},\delta_{\ell}}
with
v_{\ell}=u_{i}
for some
i\in[1,k]
, by the sequence

\xrightarrow{v_{\ell},\delta_{\ell}}\xrightarrow{w_{i,1},\delta_{\ell}}\cdots\xrightarrow{w_{i,N},\delta_{\ell}}
. It is easy to see that at the end
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime})=(q,\Lambda)
and

|\{v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{\epsilon\}\mid L^{\prime}(v)=q^{\prime}\}|\geq N
for all
q^{\prime}\in\Lambda
.

\square

D.4
Proof of
Lemma

14

Proof of
Lemma

14
.

Let
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
. First assume that there exist
two
b
-configurations
C_{in}\in\mathcal{I}
and
C
such
that
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
. Hence there exists an execution of the form
C_{0}\rightarrow C_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{n}
with
C_{0}=C_{in}
and
C_{n}=C
. Let
(q_{i},\Lambda_{i})=\mathbf{bprint}(C_{i})
for all
i\in[0,n]
. By definition of
\Rightarrow
, we have
(q_{0},\Lambda_{0})\Rightarrow(q_{1},\Lambda_{1})\Rightarrow\cdots\Rightarrow(q_{n},\Lambda_{n})
, hence
(q_{0},\Lambda_{0})\Rightarrow^{\ast}(q_{n},\Lambda_{n})
. Since
C_{0}
is an initial configuration and
(q_{0},\Lambda_{0})=\mathbf{bprint}(C_{0})
, we have
q_{0}=q_{in}
and
\Lambda_{0}\in\{\emptyset,\{q_{in}\}\}
and by definition we have
(q_{n},\Lambda_{n})=\mathbf{bprint}(C_{n})=\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
.

Now assume that
(q_{in},\Lambda_{in})\Rightarrow^{\ast}(q,\Lambda)

with
\Lambda_{in}\in\{\emptyset,\{q_{in}\}\}
. Hence we have
(q_{0},\Lambda_{0})\Rightarrow(q_{1},\Lambda_{1})\Rightarrow\cdots\Rightarrow(q_{n},\Lambda_{n})
with
(q_{0},\Lambda_{0})=(q_{in},\Lambda_{in})
and
(q_{n},\Lambda_{n})=(q,\Lambda)
. We reason by induction on
n
to show that for all
i\in[0,n]
, there exist
two
b
-configurations
C_{in}\in\mathcal{I}
and
C_{i}
such
that
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{i}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C_{i})=(q_{i},\Lambda_{i})
. If
n=0
, then it is clear that there exists an initial
b
configuration
C_{in}\in\mathcal{I}
such that
\mathbf{bprint}(C_{in})=(q_{0},\Lambda_{0})
and
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{in}
.

Suppose the property holds for
i\in[0,n-1]
and let us prove it still holds for
i+1
. By induction hypothesis, there there exist
two
b
-configurations
C_{in}\in\mathcal{I}
and
C_{i}
such
that
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{i}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C_{i})=(q_{i},\Lambda_{i})
.

Since
(q_{i},\Lambda_{i})\Rightarrow(q_{i+1},\Lambda_{i+1})
, there exists two b-configurations
C^{\prime}_{i}=((V^{\prime}_{i},E^{\prime}_{i}),L^{\prime}_{i})
and
C_{i+1}
such that
C^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow C_{i+1}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{i})=(q_{i},\Lambda_{i})
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C_{i+1})=(q_{i+1},\Lambda_{i+1})
. We let
N=\max_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{b}}(|\{v\in V^{\prime}_{i}\mid L_{i}(v)=q^{\prime}\}|)
.
Since
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C_{i}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C_{i})=(q_{i},\Lambda_{i})
, using Lemma
13
.(iii), there exists an initial configuration
C^{\prime}_{in}
and a b-configuration
C^{\prime\prime}_{i}=((V^{\prime\prime}_{i},E^{\prime\prime}_{i}),L^{\prime\prime}_{i})
such that
C^{\prime}_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime\prime}_{i}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime\prime}_{i})=(q_{i},\Lambda_{i})
and

|\{v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}\mid L^{\prime\prime}_{i}(v)=q^{\prime}\}|\geq N
for all
q^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{i}
. By definition we have
C^{\prime}_{i}\preceq C^{\prime\prime}_{i}
. By Lemma
13
.(ii), there
exists a b-configuration
C^{\prime}_{i+1}
such that
C_{i+1}\preceq C^{\prime}_{i+1}
and

\mathbf{bprint}(C_{i+1})=\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{i+1})=(q_{i+1},\Lambda_{i+1})
and
C^{\prime\prime}_{i}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{i+1}
. We deduce that we have
C^{\prime}_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{i+1}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime}_{i+1})=(q_{i+1},\Lambda_{i+1})
.

\square

D.5
Unary VASS and the control state reachability problem

We present here the syntax and semantics of (unary) Vector Addition System with States (VASS). In our context a VASS
V
is a tuple
(S,X,T)
where:
S
is a finite set of control states,
X
is a finite set of variables taking their value in the natural and
T
is a finite set of transitions of the form
(s,a,s^{\prime})
with
s,s^{\prime}\in S
and
a\in\{x++,x--\mid x\in X\}\cup\{\mathtt{skip}\}
. A configuration of such a VASS is a pair
(s,\nu)
with
s\in S
and
\nu:X\mapsto{\rm Nature}
. We define the transition relation
\vartriangleright
between VASS configurations as follows:
(s,\nu)\vartriangleright(s^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
iff there exists a transition
(s,a,s^{\prime})
in
T
one of the following condition holds:

•

a=\mathtt{skip}
and
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
, or,

•

a=x++
and
\nu^{\prime}(x)=\nu(x)+1
and
\nu^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\nu(x^{\prime})
for all
x^{\prime}\in X\setminus\{x\}
, or,

•

a=x--
and
\nu^{\prime}(x)=\nu(x)-1
and
\nu^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\nu(x^{\prime})
for all
x^{\prime}\in X\setminus\{x\}
.

Remark that if
\nu(x)=0
then it is not possible to take the transition
(s,x--,s^{\prime})
from
(s,\nu)
. We denote by
\vartriangleright^{\ast}
the reflexive and transitive closure of
\vartriangleright
. The control state reachability problem for VASS can be defined as follows:

VASSControlReach

Input:

A VASS
V=(S,X,T)
, an initial configuration
(s_{in},\nu_{in})
and
a state
s_{f}\in S
;

Question:

Does there exist
\nu^{\prime}:X\mapsto{\rm Nature}
such that
(s_{in},\nu_{in})\vartriangleright^{\ast}(s_{f},\nu^{\prime})
?

From
[
17
,
20
]
, we know that
VASSControlReach
is
ExpSpace
-complete.

D.6
Proof of
Lemma

15

Proof of
Lemma

15
.

Let
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
. From the 1-phase-bounded protocol
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
, we build a VASS
V=(S,X,T)
as follows:

•

S=Q\cup(Q\times\delta)\cup\{s_{in}\}
;

•

X=Q^{b}
;

•

T
is the smallest set verifying the following conditions:

–

(s_{in},q^{\prime}++,s_{in})\in T
for all
q^{\prime}\in\Lambda
(initialisation phase adding processes in
\Lambda
);

–

(s_{in},\mathtt{skip},q)\in T
;

–

for all
(q_{1},\tau,q_{2})\in\Delta
, we have
(q_{1},\mathtt{skip},q_{2})\in T
(central node does an internal action);

–

for all
\delta=(q_{1},\tau,q_{2})\in\Delta
with
q_{1}\in Q^{b}
and all
q^{\prime}\in Q
, we have
(q^{\prime},q_{1}--,(q^{\prime},\delta)),((q^{\prime},\delta),q_{2}++,q^{\prime})\in T
(node at height one does an internal action);

–

for all
\delta=(q_{1},!!m,q_{2})\in\Delta
and
(q^{\prime},?m,q^{\prime\prime})\in\Delta
, we have
(q^{\prime},q_{1}--,(q^{\prime\prime},\delta)),((q^{\prime\prime},\delta),q_{2}++,q^{\prime\prime})\in T
(node at height one broadcasts a message received by the root);

–

for all
\delta=(q_{1},!!m,q_{2})\in\Delta
and all
q^{\prime}\in Q
such that there deos not exist
q^{\prime\prime}
in
Q
verifying
(q^{\prime},?m,q^{\prime\prime})\in\Delta
, we have
(q^{\prime},q_{1}--,(q^{\prime},\delta)),((q^{\prime},\delta),q_{2}++,q^{\prime})\in T
(node at height one broadcasts a message not received by the root).

Intuitively, the control state of the VASS tracks the state of the root whereas the counters count how many processes are in states
Q^{b}
. One can verify that there exist a b-configuration
C
and a star-configuration

C_{f}=(\Gamma_{f},L_{f})
such that
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
and
L_{f}(\epsilon)=q_{f}

and
C\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}C_{f}
iff there exists
\nu^{\prime}:X\mapsto{\rm Nature}
such that
(s_{in},\nu_{in})\vartriangleright^{\ast}(q_{f},\nu^{\prime})
where
\nu_{in}(q)=1
for all
q\in\Lambda
and
\nu_{in}(q)=0
for all
q\in Q^{b}\setminus\Lambda
. We have hence shown that given
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
, proving whether
there exist a b-configuration
C=(\Gamma_{f},L)
and a star-configuration

C_{f}=(\Gamma_{f},L_{f})
such that
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
and
L_{f}(\epsilon)=q_{f}

and
C\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}C_{f}
reduces to the control state reachability problem for VASS,
VASSControlReach
, which is
ExpSpace
-complete.

\square

D.7
Proof of
Theorem

16

Thanks to Theorems
4
and
11
, we have to prove that given 1-phase-bounded-protocol
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
and
q_{f}\in Q
, deciding whether there exists
\Gamma\in\textsf{Stars}
,
C=(\Gamma,L)\in\mathcal{I}
and
D=(\Gamma,L^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}D
and
L^{\prime}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
is an
ExpSpace
-complete problem.

We begin with the upper bound providing an
NExpSpace
algorithm. Let
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
be a 1-phase-bounded protocol and
q_{f}\in Q
. We first guess a broadcast-print
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
and show we have
(q_{in},\Lambda_{in})\Rightarrow^{\ast}(q,\Lambda)
with
\Lambda_{in}\in\{\emptyset,\{q_{in}\}\}
. This boils down to a reachability query in the graph
(Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}},\Rightarrow)
which can be achieved in
NPSpace
thanks to
Lemma

12
and because the number of vertices in this graph is smaller than
|Q|*2^{|Q|}
. Thanks to Savitch’s theorem, we can do this reachability query in
PSpace
. Then we look for a b-configuration
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma_{f},L)
and a star-configuration

C_{f}=(\Gamma_{f},L_{f})
such that
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
and
L_{f}(\epsilon)=q_{f}

and
C\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}C_{f}
. Thanks to
Lemma

15
, this can be done in
ExpSpace
. The overall procedure gives rise to an
NExpSpace
algorithm and using again Savitch’s theorem, we obtain an
ExpSpace
-algorithm.

Let us show that this algorithm is complete. Assume there exist
\Gamma\in\textsf{Stars}
,
C=(\Gamma,L)\in\mathcal{I}
and
D=(\Gamma,L^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}D
and
L^{\prime}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
. Since the protocol is
1
-phase-bounded, there exists a b-configuration
C^{\prime}
such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}_{r}D
. If
q_{f}\notin Q^{b}
, take for
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma,L^{\prime\prime})
the last configuration in the execution
C\rightarrow^{\ast}D
such that
L^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon)\in Q^{b}
and otherwise take
C^{\prime}=D
. Thanks to Lemma
14
, if
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime})=(q,\Lambda)
, we have
(q_{in},\Lambda_{in})\Rightarrow^{\ast}(q,\Lambda)

with
\Lambda_{in}\in\{\emptyset,\{q_{in}\}\}
.-
As
L^{\prime}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
and
C^{\prime}\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}D
, we can conclude that our algorithm is complete.

We shall now show it is sound. Assume there exists
(q,\Lambda)\in Q^{b}\times 2^{Q^{b}}
such that
(q_{in},\Lambda_{in})\Rightarrow^{\ast}(q,\Lambda)

with
\Lambda_{in}\in\{\emptyset,\{q_{in}\}\}
and such that there exist a b-configuration
C^{\prime}=(\Gamma_{f},L)
and a star-configuration

C_{f}=(\Gamma_{f},L_{f})
verifying
\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime})=(q,\Lambda)
and
L_{f}(\epsilon)=q_{f}

and
C^{\prime}\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}C_{f}
. Thanks to
Lemma

14
, there exist
two
b
-configurations
C_{in}\in\mathcal{I}
and
C=((V,E),L)
in
\mathcal{C}
such
that
C_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=(q,\Lambda)
. We denote by
N=\max_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{b}}(|\{v\in V\mid L(v)=q^{\prime}\}|)
. Using
Lemma

13
.(iii), there exists an initial configuration
C^{\prime}_{in}
and a b-configuration
C^{\prime\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime})
such that
C^{\prime}_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathbf{bprint}(C)=\mathbf{bprint}(C^{\prime\prime})=(q,\Lambda)
and

|\{v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{\epsilon\}\mid L^{\prime}(v)=q^{\prime}\}|\geq N
for all
q^{\prime}\in\Lambda
. But we have then that
C^{\prime}\preceq C^{\prime\prime}
. Thanks to
Lemma

13
.(i) applied to each transition of the execution
C^{\prime}\rightarrow_{r}^{\ast}C_{f}
, we deduce that there exists
C^{\prime}_{f}=(\Gamma^{\prime}_{f},L^{\prime}_{f})
such that

C_{f}\preceq C^{\prime}_{f}
and
C^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{f}
, Since
C_{f}\preceq C^{\prime}_{f}
and
L_{f}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
we deduce that
L^{\prime}_{f}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
. Since we have
C^{\prime}_{in}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{f}
, our algorithm is sound.

It remains to prove the lower bound. For this matter, we provide a reduction from
VASSControlReach
, the control state reachability problem for VASS. The intuition being that the root node keeps track of the states in
S
and the other nodes represent the value of the counters, the value of
X
at a certain time being the number of processes in state
x_{1}
. Then the nodes encoding the counters will only perform broadcast saying whether they increment or decrement a counter and if at some point the root node receives an action on a counter, it is not suppose to do according to its control state, it will go in an error state
err
from which it will not be able to reach the final state anymore. Let
V=(S,X,T)
be a VASS,
(s_{in},\nu_{in})
an initial configuration and
s_{f}\in S
. Without loss of generality we assume that
\nu_{in}(x)=0
for all
x\in X
. We build the following broadcast protocol
P=(Q,\Sigma,q_{in},\Delta)
with:

•

Q=\{q_{in},err\}\cup S\cup\{x_{0},x_{1}\mid x\in X\}
;

•

\Sigma=\{x++,x--\mid x\in X\}
;

•

\Delta
is the smallest set satisfying the following conditions:

–

(q_{in},\tau,s_{in})
belong to
\Delta
;

–

for all
m\in\Sigma
,
(q_{in},?m,err)
belong to
\Delta
;

–

for all
x\in X
,
(q_{in},\tau,x_{0})
belong to
\Delta
;

–

(x_{0},!!x++,x_{1})
and
(x_{1},!!x--,x_{0})
are in
\Delta
for all
x\in X
;

–

for all
(s,x++,s^{\prime})\in T
, we have
(s,?x++,s^{\prime})\in\Delta
;

–

for all
(s,x--,s^{\prime})\in T
, we have
(s,?x--,s^{\prime})\in\Delta
;

–

for all
(s,\mathtt{skip},s^{\prime})\in T
, we have
(s,\tau,s^{\prime\prime})\in\Delta
;

–

for all
s\in S
and all
x\in X
, if there does not exist
s^{\prime}\in S
such that
(s,x++,s^{\prime})\in T
then
(s,?x++,err)\in\Delta
;

–

for all
s\in S
and all
x\in X
, if there does not exist
s^{\prime}\in S
such that
(s,x--,s^{\prime})\in T
then
(s,?x--,err)\in\Delta
.

Note that
P
is 1-phase bounded (the transitions leaving a state in
\{x_{0},x_{1}\mid x\in X\}
only perform broadcasts and the the transitions leaving a state in
\{s\mid s\in S\}
only perform receptions or internal actions). We can then show that there exists
\Gamma\in\textsf{Stars}
,
C=(\Gamma,L)\in\mathcal{I}
and
D=(\Gamma,L^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{P}
such that
C\rightarrow^{\ast}D
and
L^{\prime}(\epsilon)=s_{f}
iff there exists
\nu^{\prime}:X\mapsto{\rm Nature}
such that
(s_{in},\nu_{in})\vartriangleright^{\ast}(s_{f},\nu^{\prime})
.

Appendix E
Proofs of
Section

6

E.1
Proofs of
Section

6.1

We recall the definition of simple paths.
A
simple path between
u
and
u^{\prime}
in a topology
\Gamma=(V,E)
is a sequence of distinct vertices
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}
such that
u=v_{0}
,
u^{\prime}=v_{k}
, and for all
0\leq i<k
,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E
. Its length is denoted
d(v_{0},\dots,v_{k})
and is equal to
k
. In a tree topology
\Gamma^{\prime}
, for two vertices
u,u^{\prime}
, there exists a unique simple path between
u
and
u^{\prime}
, hence we denote
d(u,u^{\prime})
to denote the length of the unique path between
u
and
u^{\prime}
. Furthermore, for all vertex
u
,
d(\epsilon,u)=|u|
.

E.1.1
Proof of
Lemma

18
.

Given
(P,q_{f})
a positive instance of
{\textsc{Cover}[\textsf{Trees}]}
, we let
f(P,q_{f})
the minimal number of processes needed to cover
q_{f}
with a tree topology and we fix

\Gamma=(V,E)
a tree topology such that
|V|=f(P,q_{f})
and that covers
q_{f}
. Let
v\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
and
(C_{i}=(\Gamma,L_{i}))_{0\leq i\leq n}
configurations such that
C_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
,
C_{0}\rightarrow C_{1}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow C_{n}
and
L_{n}(v)=q_{f}
. We assume wlog that
v
is the root of the tree, i.e.
v=\epsilon
.

For all
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
, we define
b(u)
as the first index
0\leq i<n
from which
u
takes a broadcast transition, and
\infty
if it never broadcasts anything.

We define
\Gamma[u]
as the tree topology obtained from
\Gamma
by removing
u
and all
u^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
which admits
u
as a prefix. More formally,
\Gamma[u]=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
with
V^{\prime}=V\setminus\{w\in V\mid w=u\cdot w^{\prime},w^{\prime}\in{\rm Nature}^{\ast}\}
and
E^{\prime}=E\cap(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime})
.

We establish the following lemma before proving
Lemma

18
.

Lemma 45
.

For all
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus\{\epsilon\}
,
b(u)\neq\infty
. Moreover, for all
u_{1},u_{2}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus\{\epsilon\}
such that
u_{2}=u_{1}\cdot x
for some
x\in{\rm Nature}
,
it holds that
b(u_{1})>b(u_{2})
.

Proof.

We first prove the first part of the lemma.
Assume there exists
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
such that
u\neq v
and
u
does not broadcast anything during the execution. Take
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma[u]=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
. From
C^{\prime}_{0}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}
, we make the processes in
\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
perform the same sequence of transitions as in the original execution. The remaining parent of
u
can perform the same sequence of transitions as it never receives anything from
u
.

Now we prove the second part of the lemma.
Assume for the sake of contradiction that there exist
u_{1},u_{2}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)\setminus\{\epsilon\}
such that
u_{2}=u_{1}\cdot x
for some
x\in{\rm Nature}
,
and
b(u_{1})<b(u_{2})
.
From the first part of the lemma,
b(u_{1})\leq b(u_{2})<n
.
We show now that
q_{f}
is covered with
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma[u_{2}]
. For that, we will define by induction configurations
C^{\prime}_{0}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{0})
, …,
C^{\prime}_{n}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{n})
such that
C^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
,
C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}
and
C^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow^{\ast}\cdots\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{n}
. We will prove that,
for all
0\leq k\leq n
,

\displaystyle P(k):

\displaystyle\textrm{ for all $v^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{u_{1}\}$, $L^{\prime}_{k}(v^{\prime})=L_{k}(v^{\prime})$, and if $L_{k}(u_{1})\nin Q_{2}^{r}$, $L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})$}.

For
k=0
,
C^{\prime}_{0}
is uniquely defined, and obviously, for all
v^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
,
L^{\prime}_{0}(v^{\prime})=L_{0}(v^{\prime})
. Let
0\leq k<n
, and assume we have built
C^{\prime}_{0},\dots,C^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{C}
and that
P(k)
holds. Let
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
,
t\in \Delta
such that
C_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C_{k+1}
.

•

If
t=(q,\tau,q^{\prime})
, we define
L^{\prime}_{k+1}
as follows.

L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u^{\prime})=\begin{cases}q^{\prime}&\textrm{ if $u^{\prime}=u$ and $L^{\prime}_{k}(u)=L_{k}(u)$}\\
L^{\prime}_{k}(u^{\prime})&\textrm{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

First case:
L^{\prime}_{k}(u)=L_{k}(u)
. Then
L^{\prime}_{k}(u)=q
, and it follows immediately that
C^{\prime}_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C^{\prime}_{k+1}
. Also, let
v^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{u_{1}\}
. By induction hypothesis,

L^{\prime}_{k}(v^{\prime})=L_{k}(v^{\prime})
hence if
v^{\prime}\neq u
,
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(v^{\prime})=L^{\prime}_{k}(v^{\prime})=L_{k}(v^{\prime})=L_{k+1}(v^{\prime})
by definition of
C_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C_{k+1}
. Also,
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u)=L_{k+1}(u)

by definition
of
C_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C_{k+1}
. If
L_{k+1}(u_{1})\notin Q_{2}^{r}
and
u\neq u_{1}
, then
L_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k+1}(u_{1})\notin Q_{2}^{r}
, and

L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k+1}(u_{1})
. If
u=u_{1}
, then by construction
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=q^{\prime}=L_{k+1}(u_{1})
. Then
P(k+1)
holds.

Second case:
L^{\prime}_{k}(u)\neq L_{k}(u)
. Then
C^{\prime}_{k+1}=C^{\prime}_{k}
and
C^{\prime}_{k}\rightarrow^{*}C^{\prime}_{k+1}
. Moreover, since
P(k)
holds, it implies that
u=u_{1}
and
L_{k}(u_{1})\in Q_{2}^{r}
.
Then
L_{k+1}(u_{1})\in Q_{2}^{r}
and
P(k+1)
holds by induction hypothesis.

•

If
t=(q,!!m,q^{\prime})
, we differentiate three cases.

1.

if
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, and
u\neq u_{1}
, we let
C^{\prime}_{k+1}=(\Gamma^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{k+1})
defined as follows:

\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L_{k+1}(u^{\prime})\textrm{ for all $u^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{u_{1}\}$}

\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})&\textrm{ if $u_{1}\nin\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)$}\\
L_{k+1}(u_{1})&\textrm{ if $u_{1}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)$ and $L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})$}\\
p&\textrm{ if $u_{1}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)$, $L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})\neq L_{k}(u_{1})$ and there exists $(L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1}),?m,p)\in\Delta$}\\
L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})&\textrm{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

First, we show that
C^{\prime}_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C^{\prime}_{k+1}
. By induction hypothesis,
L_{k}(u^{\prime})=L^{\prime}_{k}(u^{\prime})
for all
u^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{u_{1}\}
. Then

L^{\prime}_{k}(u)=L_{k}(u)=q
and
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u)=q^{\prime}
. For all
u^{\prime}\neq u_{1}
,
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u^{\prime})=L_{k+1}(u^{\prime})
and
L_{k}(u^{\prime})=L^{\prime}_{k}(u^{\prime})
.
If
u_{1}\notin\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
, then
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})
, and
C^{\prime}_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C^{\prime}_{k+1}
. Otherwise, if
L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})
,
then
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=L_{k+1}(u_{1})
and
u_{1}
has behaved correctly, hence
C^{\prime}_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C^{\prime}_{k+1}
. If
u_{1}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
and
L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})\neq L_{k}(u_{1})
, then either there exists a transition
(L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1}),?m,p)\in\Delta
and
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=p
or
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})
.
In any case,
C^{\prime}_{k}\xrightarrow{u,t}C^{\prime}_{k+1}
.

Let
v^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{u_{1}\}
. By definition,
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(v^{\prime})=L_{k+1}(v^{\prime})
.
If
L_{k+1}(u_{1})\notin Q_{2}^{r}

then as
P
is 2-phase bounded,
L_{k}(u_{1})\nin Q_{2}^{r}
and so by induction hypothesis
L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})
.
Then, by construction of
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})
: either
u_{1}\nin\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
and so
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k+1}(u_{1})
, or
u_{1}\in\textsf{N}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(u)
and
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=L_{k+1}(u_{1})
.

2.

If
u=u_{1}
, note that, as
(L_{k}(u_{1}),!!m,L_{k+1}(u_{1}))\in\Delta
, by definition of 2-phase-bounded protocols,
L_{k}(u_{1})\nin Q_{2}^{r}
. Hence, by induction hypothesis,
L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})=q
.
We then simply let
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u^{\prime})=L_{k+1}(u^{\prime})
for all
u^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
. As
C_{k}\rightarrow C_{k+1}
, and by the induction hypothesis,

C^{\prime}_{k}\rightarrow C^{\prime}_{k+1}
. Moreover, by construction, for all
u^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
,
L^{\prime}_{k}(u^{\prime})=L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u^{\prime})
hence
P(k+1)
holds.

3.

If
u\nin\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, we let
C^{\prime}_{k+1}=C^{\prime}_{k}
and then
C^{\prime}_{k}\rightarrow^{\ast}C^{\prime}_{k+1}
. If
u\neq u_{2}
, then
\textsf{N}_{\Gamma}(u)\cap\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Indeed,
u=u_{2}\cdot w
for
w\in{\rm Nature}^{+}
, hence
u[-1]=u_{2}\cdot w^{\prime}\notin\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
and for every
x\in{\rm Nature}
,
u\cdot x\nin\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
. Hence, for
u^{\prime}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
,
L_{k+1}(u^{\prime})=L_{k}(u^{\prime})
. Then,
P(k+1)
holds.

If
u=u_{2}
, its only neighbor in
\textsf{V}(\Gamma^{\prime})
is
u_{1}
. Furthermore, as
b(u_{1})<b(u_{2})\leq k
,
u_{1}
already broadcast some messages, and as
P
is 2-phase-bounded,
L_{k}(u_{1})\in Q_{1}^{b}\cup Q_{2}^{b} \cup Q_{2}^{r}
.
Either
L_{k+1}(u_{1})=L_{k}(u_{1})
, and since
L^{\prime}_{k+1}(u_{1})=L^{\prime}_{k}(u_{1})
, the induction property still holds, or
(L_{k}(u_{1}),?m,L_{k+1}(u_{1}))\in\Delta
. Then, as
P
is 2-phase-bounded,
L_{k+1}(u_{1})\in Q_{2}^{r}
, and the induction property holds too.

Then
L^{\prime}_{n}(\epsilon)=L_{n}(\epsilon)
(since
u_{1}\neq\epsilon)
, and
L^{\prime}_{n}(\epsilon)=q_{f}
.
Hence we found a tree topology
\Gamma^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
with
|V^{\prime}|<|V|
with which
q_{f}
is coverable. This is not possible as
|V|=f(P,q_{f})
, hence
b(u_{1})>b(u_{2})
.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

18
.

Proof of
Lemma

18
.

Assume there exists
u\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
such that
|u|>|Q|+1
. For all
1\leq i\leq|u|
, we denote
v_{i}
the prefix of
u
of length
i
. By definition of a tree topology, for al
1\leq i\leq|u|
,
v_{i}\in\textsf{V}(\Gamma)
.
Let
1\leq i\leq n
, from
Lemma

45
we know that
0\leq b(v_{i})<n
. Hence, we can
denote
q_{i}
the state from which
v_{i}
performs its first broadcast, i.e.
C_{b(v_{i})}\xrightarrow{v_{i},(q_{i},!!m_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})}C_{b(v_{i})+1}
for some
m_{i}\in\Sigma
and
q^{\prime}_{i}\in Q
.

As
|u|>|Q|+1
, there exists
1<i_{1}<i_{2}\leq|u|
such that
q_{i_{1}}=q_{i_{2}}
. Note that we take two such nodes in
v_{2},\dots,v_{|u|}
instead of in
v_{1},\dots,v_{|u|}
. We will see later that we need
v_{i_{1}}
to
not have

\epsilon
as neighbor in order to apply
Lemma

45
on
v_{i_{1}}
and its neighbor
v_{i_{1}-1}
.

From
Lemma

45
,
b(v_{i_{1}})>b(v_{i_{2}})
. Take
\Gamma^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
defined as
the tree topology obtained from
\Gamma
by only keeping processes
u
such that (1) the unique path between path from
u
to
v
does not contain
v_{i_{1}}
, i.e.
v_{i_{1}}
is not a prefix of
u
, or (2) the unique path between
u
and
v
contains
v_{i_{2}}
, i.e.
v_{i_{2}}
is a prefix of
u
